<p><b>Kazakhstan</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> officially the <b>Republic of Kazakhstan</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> is a <a href="/wiki/Landlocked_country" title="Landlocked country">landlocked country</a> located mainly in <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a> and partly in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Europe" title="Eastern Europe">Eastern Europe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> It borders <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Russia_border" title="Kazakhstan–Russia border">the north and west</a>, <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a> to <a href="/wiki/China%E2%80%93Kazakhstan_border" title="China–Kazakhstan border">the east</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyzstan" title="Kyrgyzstan">Kyrgyzstan</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Kyrgyzstan_border" title="Kazakhstan–Kyrgyzstan border">the southeast</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Uzbekistan_border" title="Kazakhstan–Uzbekistan border">the south</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Turkmenistan" title="Turkmenistan">Turkmenistan</a> to <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan%E2%80%93Turkmenistan_border" title="Kazakhstan–Turkmenistan border">the southwest</a>, with a coastline along the <a href="/wiki/Caspian_Sea" title="Caspian Sea">Caspian Sea</a>. Its capital is <a href="/wiki/Astana" title="Astana">Astana</a>, known as Nur-Sultan from 2019 to 2022. <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a>, Kazakhstan's largest city, was the country's capital until 1997. Kazakhstan is the world's <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area" title="List of countries and dependencies by area">ninth-largest country by land area</a> and the largest landlocked country in the world. It has a population of 19 million people and <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population_density" title="List of countries and dependencies by population density">one of the lowest population densities</a> in the world, at fewer than 6 people&#160;per square kilometre (16 people/sq&#160;mi). Ethnic <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakhs</a> constitute a majority of the population, while <a href="/wiki/Russians_in_Kazakhstan" title="Russians in Kazakhstan">ethnic Russians</a> form a significant minority. Officially secular, Kazakhstan is a <a href="/wiki/Muslim_world" title="Muslim world">Muslim-majority country</a>, although ethnic Russians in the country form a sizeable <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Kazakhstan" title="Eastern Orthodoxy in Kazakhstan">Christian community</a>.
</p><p>The territory of Kazakhstan has historically been inhabited by nomadic groups and empires. In antiquity, the ancient <a href="/wiki/List_of_ancient_Iranian_peoples" title="List of ancient Iranian peoples">Iranic</a> nomadic <a href="/wiki/Scythians" title="Scythians">Scythians</a> inhabited the land, and the <a href="/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire" title="Achaemenid Empire">Achaemenid Persian Empire</a> expanded towards the southern territory of the modern country. <a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Turkic</a> nomads, who trace their ancestry to many Turkic states such as the <a href="/wiki/First_Turkic_Khaganate" title="First Turkic Khaganate">First Turkic Khaganate</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Second_Turkic_Khaganate" title="Second Turkic Khaganate">Second Turkic Khaganate</a>, have inhabited the country from as early as the 6th century. In the 13th century, the territory was subjugated by the <a href="/wiki/Mongol_Empire" title="Mongol Empire">Mongol Empire</a> under <a href="/wiki/Genghis_Khan" title="Genghis Khan">Genghis Khan</a>. In the 15th century, as a result of disintegration of the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate" title="Kazakh Khanate">Kazakh Khanate</a> was established on much of the lands that would later form the territory of modern Kazakhstan.
</p><p>By the 18th century, Kazakh Khanate disintegrated into three <span title="Kazakh-language text"><i lang="kk-Latn"><a href="/wiki/J%C3%BCz" title="Jüz">jüz</a></i></span> which were absorbed and conquered by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>; by the mid-19th century, the Russians nominally ruled all of Kazakhstan as part of the Russian Empire and liberated all of the slaves that the Kazakhs had captured in 1859.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Following the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Revolution" title="Russian Revolution">1917 Russian Revolution</a> and subsequent outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Civil_War" title="Russian Civil War">Russian Civil War</a>, the territory of Kazakhstan was reorganized several times. In 1936, it was established as the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic">Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic</a> within the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>. Kazakhstan was the last of the <a href="/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Republics of the Soviet Union">Soviet republics</a> to declare independence during the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Dissolution of the Soviet Union">dissolution of the Soviet Union</a> from 1988 to 1991. Human rights organizations have described the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Kazakhstan" title="Government of Kazakhstan">Kazakh government</a> as authoritarian, and regularly describe <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_Kazakhstan" title="Human rights in Kazakhstan">Kazakhstan's human rights situation</a> as poor.
</p><p>The country dominates Central Asia <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Kazakhstan" title="Economy of Kazakhstan">economically</a> and <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Kazakhstan" title="Politics of Kazakhstan">politically</a>, generating 60 percent of the region's <a href="/wiki/Gross_domestic_product" title="Gross domestic product">GDP</a>, primarily through its <a href="/wiki/Oil_and_gas_industry_in_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Oil and gas industry in Kazakhstan">oil and gas industry</a>; it also has vast mineral resources.<sup id="cite_ref-time_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time-18">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> It is <i>de jure</i> a democratic, <a href="/wiki/Unitary_state" title="Unitary state">unitary</a>, constitutional republic;<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> however, it is <i>de facto</i> an <a href="/wiki/Authoritarian_regime" class="mw-redirect" title="Authoritarian regime">authoritarian regime</a><sup id="cite_ref-index2010_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-index2010-20">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-index2022_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-index2022-21">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> with no free elections.<sup id="cite_ref-kaz2_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kaz2-22">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, there have been incremental efforts at democratization and political reform since the 2019 resignation of President <a href="/wiki/Nursultan_Nazarbayev" title="Nursultan Nazarbayev">Nursultan Nazarbayev</a>, such as imposing a presidential <a href="/wiki/Term_limit" title="Term limit">term limit</a>, decentralization, transferring more powers to Parliament, greater representational government, and the complete abolition of the <a href="/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Kazakhstan" title="Capital punishment in Kazakhstan">death penalty</a>.
</p><p>Kazakhstan has the highest <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index" title="List of countries by Human Development Index">Human Development Index</a> ranking in the region.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h2>
<p>The English word <i>Kazakh</i>, meaning a member of the Kazakh people, derives from <a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a>: <span lang="ru">казах</span>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The native name is <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a>: <span lang="kk">қазақ</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Kazakh-language romanization"><i lang="kk-Latn">qazaq</i></span>. It might originate from the Turkic word verb <i>qaz-</i>, 'to wander', reflecting the Kazakhs' <a href="/wiki/Eurasian_nomads" title="Eurasian nomads">nomadic</a> culture.<sup id="cite_ref-etym_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-etym-24">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The term '<a href="/wiki/Cossacks" title="Cossacks">Cossack</a>' is of the same origin.<sup id="cite_ref-etym_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-etym-24">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Turko-Persian" class="mw-redirect" title="Turko-Persian">Turko-Persian</a> sources, the term <i>Özbek-Qazaq</i> first appeared during the middle of the 16th century, in the <i>Tarikh-i-Rashidi</i> by <a href="/wiki/Mirza_Muhammad_Haidar_Dughlat" title="Mirza Muhammad Haidar Dughlat">Mirza Muhammad Haidar Dughlat</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Chagatai_Khan" title="Chagatai Khan">Chagatayid</a> prince of <a href="/wiki/Kashmir" title="Kashmir">Kashmir</a>. In this manuscript, the author locates Kazakh in the eastern part of <i><a href="/wiki/Desht-i_Qipchaq" class="mw-redirect" title="Desht-i Qipchaq">Desht-i Qipchaq</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Vasily_Bartold" title="Vasily Bartold">Vasily Bartold</a>, the Kazakhs likely began using that name during the 15th century.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though <i>Kazakh</i> traditionally referred only to <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">ethnic Kazakhs</a>, including those living in China, Russia, Turkey, Uzbekistan and other neighbouring countries, the term is increasingly being used to refer to any inhabitant of Kazakhstan, including residents of other ethnicities.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Kazakhstan" title="History of Kazakhstan">History of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-More_citations_needed plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Kazakhstan" title="Special:EditPage/Kazakhstan">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Kazakhstan%22">"Kazakhstan"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Kazakhstan%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Kazakhstan%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Kazakhstan%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Kazakhstan%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Kazakhstan%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">December 2018</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_(Shaded_Relief_BG).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_%28Shaded_Relief_BG%29.png/220px-Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_%28Shaded_Relief_BG%29.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="111" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_%28Shaded_Relief_BG%29.png/330px-Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_%28Shaded_Relief_BG%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_%28Shaded_Relief_BG%29.png/440px-Assimilation_of_Baltic_and_Aryan_Peoples_by_Uralic_Speakers_in_the_Middle_and_Upper_Volga_Basin_%28Shaded_Relief_BG%29.png 2x" data-file-width="1164" data-file-height="585" /></a><figcaption>Central Asia, including modern Kazakhstan, during the Iron Age.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan has been inhabited since the <a href="/wiki/Paleolithic" title="Paleolithic">Paleolithic</a> era.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Botai_culture" title="Botai culture">Botai culture</a> (3700–3100 BC) is credited with the first domestication of horses. The Botai population derived most of their ancestry from a deeply European-related population known as <a href="/wiki/Ancient_North_Eurasian" title="Ancient North Eurasian">Ancient North Eurasians</a>, while also displaying some <a href="/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians" title="Genetic history of East Asians">Ancient East Asian</a> admixture.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pastoralism" title="Pastoralism">Pastoralism</a> developed during the <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">Neolithic</a>, as the region's climate and terrain are best suited to a nomadic lifestyle. The population was <a href="/wiki/Caucasian_race" title="Caucasian race">Caucasoid</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age" title="Bronze Age">Bronze</a> and <a href="/wiki/Iron_Age" title="Iron Age">Iron Age</a> period.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Kazakh territory was a key constituent of the Eurasian trading <a href="/wiki/Steppe_Route" title="Steppe Route">Steppe Route</a>, the ancestor of the terrestrial <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road_UNESCO_World_Heritage_Sites" class="mw-redirect" title="Silk Road UNESCO World Heritage Sites">Silk Roads</a>. Archaeologists believe that humans first <a href="/wiki/Domestication_of_the_horse" title="Domestication of the horse">domesticated the horse</a> (i.e., ponies) in the region's vast steppes. During recent prehistoric times, Central Asia was inhabited by groups such as the possibly Indo-European <a href="/wiki/Afanasievo_culture" title="Afanasievo culture">Afanasievo culture</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> later early Indo-Iranian cultures such as <a href="/wiki/Andronovo_culture" title="Andronovo culture">Andronovo</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Beckwith49_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Beckwith49-33">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and later Indo-Iranians such as the <a href="/wiki/Saka" title="Saka">Saka</a> and <a href="/wiki/Massagetae" title="Massagetae">Massagetae</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-B_68_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-B_68-34">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-D_37_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-D_37-35">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Other groups included the nomadic <a href="/wiki/Scythians" title="Scythians">Scythians</a> and the Persian <a href="/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire" title="Achaemenid Empire">Achaemenid Empire</a> in the southern territory of the modern country. The Andronovo and <a href="/wiki/Srubnaya_culture" title="Srubnaya culture">Srubnaya cultures</a>, precursors to the peoples of the <a href="/wiki/Scythian_cultures" class="mw-redirect" title="Scythian cultures">Scythian cultures</a>, were found to harbor mixed ancestry from the <a href="/wiki/Yamnaya_culture" title="Yamnaya culture">Yamnaya</a> <a href="/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herders" title="Western Steppe Herders">Steppe herders</a> and peoples of the Central European Middle Neolithic.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 329 BC, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_the_Great" title="Alexander the Great">Alexander the Great</a> and his <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Macedonians" title="Ancient Macedonians">Macedonian</a> army fought in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Jaxartes" title="Battle of Jaxartes">Battle of Jaxartes</a> against the <a href="/wiki/Scythians" title="Scythians">Scythians</a> along the Jaxartes River, now known as the <a href="/wiki/Syr_Darya" title="Syr Darya">Syr Darya</a> along the southern border of modern Kazakhstan.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuman-Kipchak_and_the_Golden_Horde">Cuman-Kipchak and the Golden Horde</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Cumania" title="Cumania">Cumania</a>, <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition" title="Turco-Mongol tradition">Turco-Mongol tradition</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cumania_(1200)_eng.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Cumania_%281200%29_eng.png/220px-Cumania_%281200%29_eng.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="96" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Cumania_%281200%29_eng.png/330px-Cumania_%281200%29_eng.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Cumania_%281200%29_eng.png/440px-Cumania_%281200%29_eng.png 2x" data-file-width="1141" data-file-height="500" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Cumania" title="Cumania">Cuman–Kipchak confederation</a> in Eurasia circa 1200. The Kazakhs are descendants of <a href="/wiki/Kipchaks" title="Kipchaks">Kipchaks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nogai_Horde" title="Nogai Horde">Nogais</a> and other Turkic and medieval Mongol tribes.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The main <a href="/wiki/Turkic_migration" title="Turkic migration">migration</a> of <a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Turkic peoples</a> occurred between the 5th and 11th centuries when they spread across most of Central Asia. The Turkic peoples slowly replaced and assimilated the previous <a href="/wiki/Iranian_languages" title="Iranian languages">Iranian</a>-speaking locals, turning the population of Central Asia from largely <a href="/wiki/Indo-Iranians" title="Indo-Iranians">Iranian</a>, into primarily of East Asian descent.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/First_Turkic_Khaganate" title="First Turkic Khaganate">first Turkic Khaganate</a> was founded by <a href="/wiki/Bumin_Qaghan" title="Bumin Qaghan">Bumin</a> in 552 on the Mongolian Plateau and quickly spread west toward the Caspian Sea. The <a href="/wiki/G%C3%B6kt%C3%BCrks" title="Göktürks">Göktürks</a> drove before them various peoples: <a href="/wiki/Xionites" title="Xionites">Xionites</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uar_(tribe)" class="mw-redirect" title="Uar (tribe)">Uar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Onogurs" title="Onogurs">Oghurs</a> and others. These seem to have merged into the <a href="/wiki/Pannonian_Avars" title="Pannonian Avars">Avars</a> and <a href="/wiki/Old_Great_Bulgaria" title="Old Great Bulgaria">Bulgars</a>. Within 35 years the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Turkic_Khaganate" title="Eastern Turkic Khaganate">eastern half</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Western_Turkic_Khaganate" title="Western Turkic Khaganate">Western Turkic Khaganate</a> were independent. The Western Khaganate reached its peak in the early 7th century.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Cuman_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Cuman people">Cumans</a> entered the steppes of modern-day Kazakhstan around the early 11th century, where they later joined with the <a href="/wiki/Kipchak_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Kipchak people">Kipchak</a> and established the vast Cuman-Kipchak confederation. While ancient cities <a href="/wiki/Taraz" title="Taraz">Taraz</a> (Aulie-Ata) and <a href="/wiki/Hazrat-e_Turkestan" class="mw-redirect" title="Hazrat-e Turkestan">Hazrat-e Turkestan</a> had long served as important way-stations along the <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> connecting Asia and Europe, true political consolidation began only with the Mongol rule of the early 13th century. Under the <a href="/wiki/Mongol_Empire" title="Mongol Empire">Mongol Empire</a>, the first strictly structured administrative districts (Ulus) were established. After the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_the_Mongol_Empire" title="Division of the Mongol Empire">division of the Mongol Empire</a> in 1259, the land that would become modern-day Kazakhstan was ruled by the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a>, also known as the Ulus of Jochi. During the Golden Horde period, a <a href="/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition" title="Turco-Mongol tradition">Turco-Mongol tradition</a> emerged among the ruling elite wherein <a href="/wiki/Turkification" title="Turkification">Turkicised</a> descendants of <a href="/wiki/Genghis_Khan" title="Genghis Khan">Genghis Khan</a> followed <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> and continued to reign over the lands.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Kazakh_Khanate">Kazakh Khanate</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate" title="Kazakh Khanate">Kazakh Khanate</a></div>
<p>In 1465, the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate" title="Kazakh Khanate">Kazakh Khanate</a> emerged as a result of the dissolution of the <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a>. Established by <a href="/wiki/Janibek_Khan" title="Janibek Khan">Janibek Khan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kerei_Khan" title="Kerei Khan">Kerei Khan</a>, it continued to be ruled by the <a href="/wiki/Turco-Mongol_tradition" title="Turco-Mongol tradition">Turco-Mongol</a> clan of Tore (<a href="/wiki/Jochi" title="Jochi">Jochid</a> dynasty).<br /> Throughout this period, traditional <a href="/wiki/Nomad" title="Nomad">nomadic</a> life and a livestock-based economy continued to dominate the <a href="/wiki/Steppe" title="Steppe">steppe</a>. In the 15th century, a distinct <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakh</a> identity began to emerge among the <a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Turkic</a> tribes. This was followed by the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_War_of_Independence" title="Kazakh War of Independence">Kazakh War of Independence</a>, where the Khanate gained its sovereignty from the <a href="/wiki/Shaybanids" title="Shaybanids">Shaybanids</a>. The process was consolidated by the mid-16th century with the appearance of the Kazakh <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">language</a>, culture, and economy.
</p>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg/250px-%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg/375px-%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg/500px-%D0%96%D1%83%D0%B7.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="658" data-file-height="369" /></a><figcaption>Approximate areas occupied by the three Kazakh jüz in the early 20th century <table> <tbody><tr> <td><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#ccff99; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Junior_Juz" class="mw-redirect" title="Junior Juz">Junior Juz</a></div> </td> <td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#fdd99b; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Middle_Juz" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle Juz">Middle Juz</a></div> </td> <td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#ffaaaa; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/w/index.php?title=Great_Juz&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Great Juz (page does not exist)">Great Juz</a></div> </td></tr></tbody></table></figcaption></figure>
<p>Nevertheless, the region was the focus of ever-increasing disputes between the native Kazakh <a href="/wiki/Emir" title="Emir">emirs</a> and the neighbouring <a href="/wiki/Persian_language" title="Persian language">Persian-speaking peoples</a> to the south. At its height, the Khanate would rule parts of Central Asia and control <a href="/wiki/Cumania" title="Cumania">Cumania</a>. The Kazakh Khanate's territories would expand deep into Central Asia. By the early 17th century, the Kazakh Khanate was struggling with the impact of tribal rivalries, which had effectively divided the population into the Great, Middle and Little (or Small) hordes (<i><a href="/wiki/J%C3%BCz" title="Jüz">jüz</a></i>). Political disunion, tribal rivalries, and the diminishing importance of overland trade routes between east and west weakened the Kazakh Khanate. The <a href="/wiki/Khiva_Khanate" class="mw-redirect" title="Khiva Khanate">Khiva Khanate</a> used this opportunity and annexed the <a href="/wiki/Mangyshlak_Peninsula" title="Mangyshlak Peninsula">Mangyshlak Peninsula</a>. Uzbek rule there lasted two centuries until the Russian arrival.
</p><p>During the 17th century, the Kazakhs fought the <a href="/wiki/Oirats" title="Oirats">Oirats</a>, a federation of western <a href="/wiki/Mongols" title="Mongols">Mongol</a> tribes, including the <a href="/wiki/Dzungar_people" title="Dzungar people">Dzungar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The beginning of the 18th century marked the zenith of the Kazakh Khanate. During this period the Little Horde participated in the 1723–1730 <a href="/wiki/Kazakh-Dzungar_Wars" class="mw-redirect" title="Kazakh-Dzungar Wars">war against the Dzungar Khanate</a>, following their "Great Disaster" invasion of Kazakh territory. Under the leadership of <a href="/wiki/Abul_Khair_Khan" title="Abul Khair Khan">Abul Khair Khan</a>, the Kazakhs won major victories over the Dzungar at the Bulanty River in 1726 and at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_A%C3%B1yraqai" title="Battle of Añyraqai">Battle of Añyraqai</a> in 1729.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ablai_Khan" title="Ablai Khan">Ablai Khan</a> participated in the most significant battles against the Dzungar from the 1720s to the 1750s, for which he was declared a "<i>batyr</i>" ("hero") by the people. The Kazakhs suffered from the frequent raids against them by the Volga <a href="/wiki/Kalmyks" title="Kalmyks">Kalmyks</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Kokand_Khanate" class="mw-redirect" title="Kokand Khanate">Kokand Khanate</a> used the weakness of Kazakh jüzs after Dzungar and Kalmyk raids and conquered present Southeastern Kazakhstan, including <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a>, the formal capital in the first quarter of the 19th century. Also, the <a href="/wiki/Emirate_of_Bukhara" title="Emirate of Bukhara">Emirate of Bukhara</a> ruled <a href="/wiki/Shymkent" title="Shymkent">Shymkent</a> before the Russians gained dominance.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Russian_Kazakhstan">Russian Kazakhstan</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG/220px-Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG/330px-Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG/440px-Battle_Cossacks_with_Kyrgyz_1826.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1349" data-file-height="915" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ural_Cossacks" title="Ural Cossacks">Ural Cossacks</a> skirmish with Kazakhs</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg/220px-%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="108" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg/330px-%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg/440px-%D0%9A%D0%B0%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B3%D0%B8%D0%B7%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B3%D0%BE_%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%8F.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4884" data-file-height="2401" /></a><figcaption>Map of the Kazakh Territory in 1903</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg/220px-Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="338" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg/330px-Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg/440px-Kazakhs_19th_Century_4.jpg 2x" data-file-width="520" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Kazakh woman in wedding clothes, 19th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the first half of the 18th century, the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> constructed the Irtysh line, a series of forty-six forts and ninety-six redoubts, including <a href="/wiki/Omsk" title="Omsk">Omsk</a> (1716), <a href="/wiki/Semey" title="Semey">Semipalatinsk</a> (1718), <a href="/wiki/Pavlodar" title="Pavlodar">Pavlodar</a> (1720), <a href="/wiki/Orenburg" title="Orenburg">Orenburg</a> (1743) and <a href="/wiki/Petropavl" title="Petropavl">Petropavlovsk</a> (1752),<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> to prevent Kazakh and Oirat raids into Russian territory.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In the late 18th century the Kazakhs took advantage of <a href="/wiki/Pugachev%27s_Rebellion" title="Pugachev&#39;s Rebellion">Pugachev's Rebellion</a>, which was centred on the Volga area, to raid Russian and <a href="/wiki/Volga_Germans" title="Volga Germans">Volga German</a> settlements.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In the 19th century, the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> began to expand its influence into Central Asia. The "<a href="/wiki/Great_Game" title="Great Game">Great Game</a>" period is generally regarded as running from approximately 1813 to the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Russian_Convention_of_1907" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Russian Convention of 1907">Anglo-Russian Convention of 1907</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Tsar" title="Tsar">tsars</a> effectively ruled over most of the territory belonging to what is now the Republic of Kazakhstan.
</p><p>The Russian Empire introduced a system of administration and built military garrisons and barracks in its effort to establish a presence in Central Asia in the so-called "Great Game" for dominance in the area against the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>, which was extending its influence from the south in India and Southeast Asia. Russia built its first outpost, <a href="/wiki/Orsk" title="Orsk">Orsk</a>, in 1735. Russia introduced the Russian language in all schools and governmental organisations.
</p><p>Russia's efforts to impose its system aroused the resentment of the <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakh people</a>, and, by the 1860s, some Kazakhs resisted its rule. Russia had disrupted the traditional nomadic lifestyle and livestock-based economy, and people were suffering from hunger and starvation, with some Kazakh tribes being decimated. The Kazakh national movement, which began in the late 19th century, sought to preserve the native language and identity by resisting the attempts of the Russian Empire to assimilate and stifle Kazakh culture.
</p><p>From the 1890s onward, ever-larger numbers of settlers from the Russian Empire began <a href="/wiki/Colonization" title="Colonization">colonizing</a> the territory of present-day Kazakhstan, in particular, the province of <a href="/wiki/Semirechye" class="mw-redirect" title="Semirechye">Semirechye</a>. The number of settlers rose still further once the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Aral_Railway" title="Trans-Aral Railway">Trans-Aral Railway</a> from <a href="/wiki/Orenburg" title="Orenburg">Orenburg</a> to <a href="/wiki/Tashkent" title="Tashkent">Tashkent</a> was completed in 1906. A specially created Migration Department (Переселенческое Управление) in <a href="/wiki/St._Petersburg" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Petersburg">St. Petersburg</a> oversaw and encouraged the migration to expand Russian influence in the area. During the 19th century, about 400,000 Russians immigrated to Kazakhstan, and about one million Slavs, Germans, Jews, and others immigrated to the region during the first third of the 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Vasile_Balabanov" class="mw-redirect" title="Vasile Balabanov">Vasile Balabanov</a> was the administrator responsible for the resettlement during much of this time.
</p><p>The competition for land and water that ensued between the Kazakhs and the newcomers caused great resentment against colonial rule during the final years of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>. The most serious uprising, the <a href="/wiki/Central_Asian_revolt_of_1916" title="Central Asian revolt of 1916">Central Asian revolt</a>, occurred in 1916. The Kazakhs attacked Russian and <a href="/wiki/Cossacks" title="Cossacks">Cossack</a> settlers and military garrisons. The revolt resulted in a series of clashes and in brutal massacres committed by both sides.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Both sides resisted the communist government until late 1919.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Kazakh_SSR">Kazakh SSR</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic">Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg/220px-Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="154" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg/330px-Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg/440px-Stanitsa_Sofiiskaya.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="561" /></a><figcaption>Stanitsa Sofiiskaya, <a href="/wiki/Talgar" title="Talgar">Talgar</a>, 1920s</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg/220px-Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg/330px-Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg/440px-Young_Pioneers_in_Kazakh_SSR.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="762" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Young_Pioneer_organization_of_the_Soviet_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Young Pioneer organization of the Soviet Union">Young Pioneers</a> at a Young Pioneer camp in the Kazakh SSR</figcaption></figure>
<p>Following the <a href="/wiki/October_Revolution" title="October Revolution">collapse of central government</a> in <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Petrograd</a> in November 1917, the Kazakhs (then in Russia officially referred to as "Kirghiz") experienced a brief period of <a href="/wiki/Autonomous_entity" class="mw-redirect" title="Autonomous entity">autonomy</a> (the <a href="/wiki/Alash_Autonomy" title="Alash Autonomy">Alash Autonomy</a>) before eventually succumbing to the <a href="/wiki/Bolshevik" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolshevik">Bolsheviks</a>′ rule. On 26 August 1920, the <a href="/wiki/Kirghiz_Autonomous_Socialist_Soviet_Republic_(1920%E2%80%9325)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kirghiz Autonomous Socialist Soviet Republic (1920–25)">Kirghiz Autonomous Socialist Soviet Republic</a> within the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic" title="Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic">Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic</a> (RSFSR) was established. The Kirghiz ASSR included the territory of present-day Kazakhstan, but its administrative centre was the mainly Russian-populated town of <a href="/wiki/Orenburg" title="Orenburg">Orenburg</a>. In June 1925, the Kirghiz ASSR was renamed the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Kazakh Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic">Kazak ASSR</a> and its administrative centre was transferred to the town of <a href="/wiki/Kyzylorda" title="Kyzylorda">Kyzylorda</a>, and in April 1927 to <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Alma-Ata</a>.
</p><p>Soviet repression of the traditional elite, along with forced <a href="/wiki/Collectivisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Collectivisation">collectivisation</a> in the late 1920s and 1930s, brought <a href="/wiki/Famine" title="Famine">famine</a> and high fatalities, leading to unrest (see also: <a href="/wiki/Famine_in_Kazakhstan_of_1932%E2%80%9333" class="mw-redirect" title="Famine in Kazakhstan of 1932–33">Famine in Kazakhstan of 1932–33</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> During the 1930s, some members of the Kazakh intelligentsia were executed – as part of the <a href="/wiki/Political_repression_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Political repression in the Soviet Union">policies of political reprisals</a> pursued by the Soviet government in Moscow.
</p><p>On 5 December 1936, the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Kazakh Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic">Kazakh Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic</a> (whose territory by then corresponded to that of modern Kazakhstan) was detached from the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic" title="Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic">Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic</a> (RSFSR) and made the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic">Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic</a>, a full <a href="/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Republics of the Soviet Union">union republic</a> of the USSR, one of eleven such republics at the time, along with the <a href="/wiki/Kirghiz_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Kirghiz Soviet Socialist Republic">Kirghiz Soviet Socialist Republic</a>.
</p><p>The republic was one of the destinations for exiled and convicted persons, as well as for mass resettlements, or deportations affected by the central USSR authorities during the 1930s and 1940s, such as approximately 400,000 <a href="/wiki/Volga_Germans" title="Volga Germans">Volga Germans</a> deported from the <a href="/wiki/Volga_German_Autonomous_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Volga German Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic">Volga German Autonomous Soviet Socialist Republic</a> in September–October 1941, and then later the <a href="/wiki/Greeks_in_Kazakhstan" title="Greeks in Kazakhstan">Greeks</a> and <a href="/wiki/Deportation_of_the_Crimean_Tatars" title="Deportation of the Crimean Tatars">Crimean Tatars</a>. Deportees and prisoners were interned in some of the biggest <a href="/wiki/Gulag" title="Gulag">Soviet labour camps</a> (the Gulag), including <a href="/wiki/Akmol" title="Akmol">ALZhIR</a> camp outside Astana, which was reserved for the wives of men considered "enemies of the people".<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Many moved due to the policy of <a href="/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Population transfer in the Soviet Union">population transfer in the Soviet Union</a> and others were forced into <a href="/wiki/Involuntary_settlements_in_the_Soviet_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Involuntary settlements in the Soviet Union">involuntary settlements in the Soviet Union</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:International_conference_on_Primary_Health_Care_-_Conferencia_Internacional_sobre_Atenci%C3%B3n_Primaria_de_Salud_-_Almaty_-1978.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/International_conference_on_Primary_Health_Care_-_Conferencia_Internacional_sobre_Atenci%C3%B3n_Primaria_de_Salud_-_Almaty_-1978.jpg/220px-International_conference_on_Primary_Health_Care_-_Conferencia_Internacional_sobre_Atenci%C3%B3n_Primaria_de_Salud_-_Almaty_-1978.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/International_conference_on_Primary_Health_Care_-_Conferencia_Internacional_sobre_Atenci%C3%B3n_Primaria_de_Salud_-_Almaty_-1978.jpg/330px-International_conference_on_Primary_Health_Care_-_Conferencia_Internacional_sobre_Atenci%C3%B3n_Primaria_de_Salud_-_Almaty_-1978.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fd/International_conference_on_Primary_Health_Care_-_Conferencia_Internacional_sobre_Atenci%C3%B3n_Primaria_de_Salud_-_Almaty_-1978.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="282" /></a><figcaption>The International Conference on Primary Health Care in 1978, known as the <a href="/wiki/Alma_Ata_Declaration" title="Alma Ata Declaration">Alma-Ata Declaration</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Front_(World_War_II)" title="Eastern Front (World War II)">Soviet-German War</a> (1941–1945) led to an increase in industrialisation and <a href="/wiki/Mineral_extraction" class="mw-redirect" title="Mineral extraction">mineral extraction</a> in support of the war effort. At the time of <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Stalin" title="Joseph Stalin">Joseph Stalin</a>'s death in 1953, however, Kazakhstan still had an overwhelmingly agricultural economy. In 1953, Soviet leader <a href="/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev" title="Nikita Khrushchev">Nikita Khrushchev</a> initiated the <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Lands_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Lands Campaign">Virgin Lands Campaign</a> designed to turn the traditional pasturelands of Kazakhstan into a major grain-producing region for the Soviet Union. The Virgin Lands policy brought mixed results. However, along with later modernisations under Soviet leader <a href="/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev" title="Leonid Brezhnev">Leonid Brezhnev</a> (in power 1964–1982), it accelerated the development of the agricultural sector, which remains the source of livelihood for a large percentage of Kazakhstan's population. Because of the decades of privation, war and resettlement, by 1959 the <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakhs</a> had become a minority in the country, making up 30 percent of the population. Ethnic <a href="/wiki/Russians" title="Russians">Russians</a> accounted for 43 percent.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1947, the USSR government, as part of its <a href="/wiki/Soviet_atomic_bomb_project" title="Soviet atomic bomb project">atomic bomb project</a>, founded an <a href="/wiki/Semipalatinsk_Test_Site" title="Semipalatinsk Test Site">atomic bomb test site</a> near the north-eastern town of <a href="/wiki/Semey" title="Semey">Semipalatinsk</a>, where the <a href="/wiki/RDS-1" title="RDS-1">first Soviet nuclear bomb</a> test was conducted in 1949. Hundreds of nuclear tests were conducted until 1989 with adverse consequences for the nation's environment and population.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Anti-nuclear_movement_in_Kazakhstan" title="Anti-nuclear movement in Kazakhstan">Anti-nuclear movement in Kazakhstan</a> became a major political force in the late 1980s.
</p><p>In December 1986, mass demonstrations by young ethnic Kazakhs, later called the <a href="/wiki/Jeltoqsan" title="Jeltoqsan">Jeltoqsan</a> riot, took place in Almaty to protest the replacement of the <a href="/wiki/General_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="General Secretary">First Secretary</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Kazakhstan" title="Communist Party of Kazakhstan">Communist Party</a> of the Kazakh SSR <a href="/wiki/Dinmukhamed_Konayev" class="mw-redirect" title="Dinmukhamed Konayev">Dinmukhamed Konayev</a> with <a href="/wiki/Gennady_Kolbin" title="Gennady Kolbin">Gennady Kolbin</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Russian_SFSR" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian SFSR">Russian SFSR</a>. Governmental troops suppressed the unrest, several people were killed, and many demonstrators were jailed.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> In the waning days of Soviet rule, discontent continued to grow and found expression under Soviet leader <a href="/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev" title="Mikhail Gorbachev">Mikhail Gorbachev</a>'s policy of <i><a href="/wiki/Glasnost" title="Glasnost">glasnost</a></i> ("openness").
</p><p><span class="anchor" id="Independence"></span>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Independence">Independence</span></h3>
<p>On 25 October 1990, Kazakhstan declared its <a href="/wiki/Sovereignty" title="Sovereignty">sovereignty</a> on its territory as a republic within the Soviet Union. Following the August 1991 aborted <a href="/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_attempt" title="1991 Soviet coup d&#39;état attempt">coup attempt</a> in Moscow, Kazakhstan declared <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstani_Independence_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="Kazakhstani Independence Day">independence</a> on 16 December 1991, thus becoming the last Soviet republic to declare independence. Ten days later, the Soviet Union itself <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Dissolution of the Soviet Union">ceased to exist</a>.
</p><p>Kazakhstan's communist-era leader, <a href="/wiki/Nursultan_Nazarbayev" title="Nursultan Nazarbayev">Nursultan Nazarbayev</a>, became the country's first President. Nazarbayev ruled in an authoritarian manner. An emphasis was placed on converting the country's economy to a market economy while political reforms lagged behind economic advances. By 2006, Kazakhstan was generating 60 percent of the GDP of Central Asia, primarily through its oil industry.<sup id="cite_ref-time_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time-18">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1997, the government moved the capital to <a href="/wiki/Astana" title="Astana">Astana</a>, renamed Nur-Sultan on 23 March 2019,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> from <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a>, Kazakhstan's largest city, where it had been established under the Soviet Union.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 2019, Nazarbayev resigned 29 years after taking office. However, he continued to lead the influential security council and held the formal title Leader of the Nation.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Kassym-Jomart_Tokayev" title="Kassym-Jomart Tokayev">Kassym-Jomart Tokayev</a> succeeded Nazarbayev as the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Kazakhstan" title="President of Kazakhstan">President of Kazakhstan</a>. His first official act was to rename the capital after his predecessor.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In June 2019, the new president, Kassym-Jomart Tokayev, won Kazakhstan's presidential <a href="/wiki/2019_Kazakh_presidential_election" title="2019 Kazakh presidential election">election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2022, the country plunged into <a href="/wiki/2022_Kazakhstan_unrest" class="mw-redirect" title="2022 Kazakhstan unrest">political unrest</a> following a spike in fuel prices.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In consequence, President Kassym-Jomart Tokayev took over as head of the powerful Security Council, removing his predecessor Nursultan Nazarbayev from the post.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> In September 2022, the name of the country's capital was changed back to Astana from Nur-Sultan.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Kazakhstan" title="Geography of Kazakhstan">Geography of Kazakhstan</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Kazakhstan" title="List of cities in Kazakhstan">List of cities in Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg/220px-Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg/330px-Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg/440px-Kazakhstan_BMNG.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3803" data-file-height="2179" /></a><figcaption>Satellite image of Kazakhstan (November 2004)</figcaption></figure>
<p>As it extends across both sides of the <a href="/wiki/Ural_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ural River">Ural River</a>, considered the dividing line separating Europe and Asia, Kazakhstan is one of only two <a href="/wiki/Landlocked_country" title="Landlocked country">landlocked countries</a> in the world that <a href="/wiki/Transcontinental_countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Transcontinental countries">has territory in two continents</a> (the other is <a href="/wiki/Azerbaijan" title="Azerbaijan">Azerbaijan</a>).
</p><p>With an area of 2,700,000 square kilometres (1,000,000&#160;sq&#160;mi)&#160;&#8211;&#32;equivalent in size to Western Europe&#160;&#8211;&#32;Kazakhstan is the ninth-largest country and largest landlocked country in the world. While it was part of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>, Kazakhstan lost some of its territory to China's <a href="/wiki/Xinjiang" title="Xinjiang">Xinjiang</a> province,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> and some to Uzbekistan's <a href="/wiki/Karakalpakstan" title="Karakalpakstan">Karakalpakstan</a> autonomous republic during Soviet years.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg/220px-Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg/330px-Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg/440px-Eurasian_steppe_belt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1529" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Steppe" title="Kazakh Steppe">Kazakh Steppe</a> is part of the <a href="/wiki/Eurasian_Steppe" title="Eurasian Steppe">Eurasian Steppe</a> Belt (in <span style="display:inline-block;width:1em;vertical-align: text-top;height:1em;border:1.62px solid black;background:#AAEEFF;" title="#aaeeff">&#8195;</span> on the map).</figcaption></figure>
<p>It shares borders of 6,846 kilometres (4,254&#160;mi) with Russia, 2,203 kilometres (1,369&#160;mi) with <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a>, 1,533 kilometres (953&#160;mi) with China, 1,051 kilometres (653&#160;mi) with <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyzstan" title="Kyrgyzstan">Kyrgyzstan</a>, and 379 kilometres (235&#160;mi) with <a href="/wiki/Turkmenistan" title="Turkmenistan">Turkmenistan</a>. Major cities include <a href="/wiki/Astana" title="Astana">Astana</a>, <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a>, <a href="/wiki/Karagandy" class="mw-redirect" title="Karagandy">Karagandy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shymkent" title="Shymkent">Shymkent</a>, <a href="/wiki/Atyrau" title="Atyrau">Atyrau</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Oskemen" title="Oskemen">Oskemen</a>. It lies between latitudes <a href="/wiki/40th_parallel_north" title="40th parallel north">40°</a> and <a href="/wiki/56th_parallel_north" title="56th parallel north">56° N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/46th_meridian_east" title="46th meridian east">46°</a> and <a href="/wiki/88th_meridian_east" title="88th meridian east">88° E</a>. While located primarily in Asia, a small portion of Kazakhstan is also located west of the <a href="/wiki/Urals" class="mw-redirect" title="Urals">Urals</a> in Eastern Europe.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kazakhstan's terrain extends west to east from the <a href="/wiki/Caspian_Sea" title="Caspian Sea">Caspian Sea</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Altay_Mountains" class="mw-redirect" title="Altay Mountains">Altay Mountains</a> and north to south from the plains of <a href="/wiki/Siberia" title="Siberia">Western Siberia</a> to the oases and deserts of Central Asia. The <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Steppe" title="Kazakh Steppe">Kazakh Steppe</a> (plain), with an area of around 804,500 square kilometres (310,600&#160;sq&#160;mi), occupies one-third of the country and is the world's largest dry <a href="/wiki/Steppe" title="Steppe">steppe</a> region. The steppe is characterised by large areas of <a href="/wiki/Grassland" title="Grassland">grasslands</a> and sandy regions. Major seas, lakes and rivers include <a href="/wiki/Lake_Balkhash" title="Lake Balkhash">Lake Balkhash</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lake_Zaysan" title="Lake Zaysan">Lake Zaysan</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Charyn_Canyon" title="Charyn Canyon">Charyn River and gorge</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Ili_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ili River">Ili</a>, <a href="/wiki/Irtysh_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Irtysh River">Irtysh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ishim_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ishim River">Ishim</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ural_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Ural River">Ural</a> and <a href="/wiki/Syr_Darya" title="Syr Darya">Syr Darya</a> rivers, and the <a href="/wiki/Aral_Sea" title="Aral Sea">Aral Sea</a> until it largely dried up in one of the world's worst environmental disasters.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Charyn_Canyon" title="Charyn Canyon">Charyn Canyon</a> is 80 kilometres (50&#160;mi) long, cutting through a red <a href="/wiki/Sandstone" title="Sandstone">sandstone</a> plateau and stretching along the Charyn River gorge in northern <a href="/wiki/Tian_Shan" title="Tian Shan">Tian Shan</a> ("Heavenly Mountains", 200&#160;km (124&#160;mi) east of Almaty) at <span class="geo-inline"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1156832818"><span class="plainlinks nourlexpansion"><a class="external text" href="https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Kazakhstan&amp;params=43_21_1.16_N_79_4_49.28_E_"><span class="geo-default"><span class="geo-dms" title="Maps, aerial photos, and other data for this location"><span class="latitude">43°21′1.16″N</span> <span class="longitude">79°4′49.28″E</span></span></span><span class="geo-multi-punct">&#xfeff; / &#xfeff;</span><span class="geo-nondefault"><span class="geo-dec" title="Maps, aerial photos, and other data for this location">43.3503222°N 79.0803556°E</span><span style="display:none">&#xfeff; / <span class="geo">43.3503222; 79.0803556</span></span></span></a></span></span>. The steep canyon slopes, columns and arches rise to heights of between 150 and 300 metres (490 and 980 feet). The inaccessibility of the canyon provided a safe haven for a rare <a href="/wiki/Ash_tree" class="mw-redirect" title="Ash tree">ash tree</a>, <i>Fraxinus sogdiana</i>, which survived the <a href="/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period" title="Last Glacial Period">Ice Age</a> there and has now also grown in some other areas.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2014)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/wiki/Bigach_crater" title="Bigach crater">Bigach crater</a>, at <span class="geo-inline"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1156832818"><span class="plainlinks nourlexpansion"><a class="external text" href="https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Kazakhstan&amp;params=48_30_N_82_00_E_"><span class="geo-default"><span class="geo-dms" title="Maps, aerial photos, and other data for this location"><span class="latitude">48°30′N</span> <span class="longitude">82°00′E</span></span></span><span class="geo-multi-punct">&#xfeff; / &#xfeff;</span><span class="geo-nondefault"><span class="geo-dec" title="Maps, aerial photos, and other data for this location">48.500°N 82.000°E</span><span style="display:none">&#xfeff; / <span class="geo">48.500; 82.000</span></span></span></a></span></span>, is a <a href="/wiki/Pliocene" title="Pliocene">Pliocene</a> or <a href="/wiki/Miocene" title="Miocene">Miocene</a> <a href="/wiki/Asteroid" title="Asteroid">asteroid</a> <a href="/wiki/Impact_crater" title="Impact crater">impact crater</a>, 8&#160;km (5&#160;mi) in diameter and estimated to be 5±3&#160;million years old.
</p><p>Kazakhstan's <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a> region is also home to the <a href="/wiki/Mynzhylky_mountain_plateau" class="mw-redirect" title="Mynzhylky mountain plateau">Mynzhylky mountain plateau</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Natural_resources">Natural resources</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Energy_policy_of_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Energy policy of Kazakhstan">Energy policy of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg/220px-Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg/330px-Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg/440px-Reserve_Karkaraly.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Karaganda_Region" title="Karaganda Region">Karaganda Region</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan has an abundant supply of accessible mineral and fossil fuel resources. Development of petroleum, natural gas, and mineral extractions has attracted most of the over $40&#160;billion in foreign investment in Kazakhstan since 1993 and accounts for some 57 percent of the nation's industrial output (or approximately 13 percent of gross domestic product). According to some estimates,<sup id="cite_ref-Homestead_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Homestead-62">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Kazakhstan has the second largest <a href="/wiki/Uranium" title="Uranium">uranium</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chromium" title="Chromium">chromium</a>, lead, and <a href="/wiki/Zinc" title="Zinc">zinc</a> reserves; the third largest <a href="/wiki/Manganese" title="Manganese">manganese</a> reserves; the fifth largest copper reserves; and ranks in the top ten for coal, iron, and gold. It is also an exporter of diamonds. Perhaps most significant for economic development, Kazakhstan also has the 11th largest proven reserves of both petroleum and natural gas.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> One such location is the <a href="/wiki/Tokarevskoye_gas_condensate_field" title="Tokarevskoye gas condensate field">Tokarevskoye gas condensate field</a>.
</p><p>In total, there are 160 deposits with over 2.7&#160;billion tonnes (2.7&#160;billion long tons) of petroleum. Oil explorations have shown that the deposits on the <a href="/wiki/Caspian_sea" class="mw-redirect" title="Caspian sea">Caspian shore</a> are only a small part of a much larger deposit. It is said that 3.5&#160;billion tonnes (3.4&#160;billion long tons) of oil and 2.5&#160;billion cubic metres (88&#160;billion cubic feet) of gas could be found in that area. Overall the estimate of Kazakhstan's oil deposits is 6.1&#160;billion tonnes (6.0&#160;billion long tons). However, there are only three <a href="/wiki/Oil_refinery" title="Oil refinery">refineries</a> within the country, situated in <a href="/wiki/Atirau" class="mw-redirect" title="Atirau">Atyrau</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Pavlodar" title="Pavlodar">Pavlodar</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Shymkent" title="Shymkent">Shymkent</a>. These are not capable of processing the total crude output, so much of it is exported to Russia. According to the US <a href="/wiki/Energy_Information_Administration" title="Energy Information Administration">Energy Information Administration</a>, Kazakhstan was producing approximately 1,540,000 barrels (245,000&#160;m<sup>3</sup>) of oil per day in 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kazakhstan also possesses large deposits of <a href="/wiki/Phosphorite" title="Phosphorite">phosphorite</a>. Two of the largest deposits include the Karatau basin with 650 million tonnes of P<sub>2</sub>O<sub>5</sub> and the Chilisai deposit of the <a href="https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%90%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%BE%D0%B1%D0%B8%D0%BD%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D1%84%D0%BE%D1%81%D1%84%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%BE%D0%BD%D0%BE%D1%81%D0%BD%D1%8B%D0%B9_%D0%B1%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D0%B5%D0%B9%D0%BD" class="extiw" title="ru:Актобинский фосфоритоносный бассейн">Aqtobe phosphorite basin</a> located in northwestern Kazakhstan, with resources of 500–800<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>million tonnes of 9 percent ore.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 17 October 2013, the <a href="/wiki/Extractive_Industries_Transparency_Initiative" title="Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative">Extractive Industries Transparency Initiative</a> (EITI) accepted Kazakhstan as "EITI Compliant", meaning that the country has a basic and functional process to ensure the regular disclosure of natural resource revenues.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg/220px-Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg/330px-Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg/440px-Kazakhstan_map_of_K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1900" data-file-height="1900" /></a><figcaption>Kazakhstan map of Köppen climate classification</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan has an "extreme" <a href="/wiki/Continental_climate" title="Continental climate">continental climate</a>, with hot summers and very cold winters. Indeed, Astana is the second coldest capital city in the world after <a href="/wiki/Ulaanbaatar" title="Ulaanbaatar">Ulaanbaatar</a>. <a href="/wiki/Precipitation_(meteorology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Precipitation (meteorology)">Precipitation</a> varies between arid and semi-arid conditions, the winter being particularly dry.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
