2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="1675" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Lake_Burabay" title="Lake Burabay">Lake Burabay</a>, view from Mount Bolectau</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG/220px-%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG/330px-%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG/440px-%D0%92%D0%B5%D1%80%D1%85%D0%BD%D1%8F%D1%8F_%D1%82%D0%BE%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B0_%D0%B3%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%BB%D1%8B%D0%B6%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B9_%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%81%D1%8B_%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%80%D0%BE%D1%80%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%A7%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B1%D1%83%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%BA..JPG 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Shymbulak" title="Shymbulak">Shymbulak</a> ski resort in Almaty</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan is the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area" title="List of countries and dependencies by area">ninth-largest country by area</a> and the largest landlocked country in the world. As of 2014, tourism accounted for 0.3 percent of Kazakhstan's GDP, but the government had plans to increase it to 3 percent by 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-theguardian1_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-theguardian1-200">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Travel_and_Tourism_Competitiveness_Report" title="Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Report">Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Report</a> of 2017, travel and tourism industry GDP in Kazakhstan was $3.08&#160;billion or only 1.6 percent of total GDP. The WEF ranked Kazakhstan 80th in its 2019 report.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2017, Kazakhstan ranked 43rd in the number of tourist arrivals. In 2014, <a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a> described tourism in Kazakhstan as, "hugely underdeveloped", despite the country's mountain, lake and desert landscapes.<sup id="cite_ref-Lillis_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lillis-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> Factors hampering an increase in tourism were said to include high prices, "shabby infrastructure", "poor service" and the difficulties of travel in a large underdeveloped country.<sup id="cite_ref-Lillis_203-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lillis-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> Even for Kazakhs, going for a holiday abroad may cost only half the price of taking a holiday in Kazakhstan.<sup id="cite_ref-Lillis_203-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lillis-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Kazakh Government, long characterised as authoritarian with a history of human rights abuses and suppression of political opposition,<sup id="cite_ref-time_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time-18">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> in 2015 issued a "Tourism Industry Development Plan 2020." It aimed to establish five tourism clusters in Kazakhstan: <a href="/wiki/Astana" title="Astana">Astana</a> city, <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a> city, <a href="/wiki/East_Kazakhstan_Region" title="East Kazakhstan Region">East Kazakhstan</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Kazakhstan_Region" class="mw-redirect" title="South Kazakhstan Region">South Kazakhstan</a>, and <a href="/wiki/West_Kazakhstan_Region" title="West Kazakhstan Region">West Kazakhstan</a> Oblasts. It also sought investment of $4&#160;billion and the creation of 300,000 new jobs in the tourism industry by 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Lillis_203-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lillis-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kazakhstan has offered a permanent visa-free regime for up to 90 days to citizens of <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Azerbaijan" title="Azerbaijan">Azerbaijan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Belarus" title="Belarus">Belarus</a>, Georgia, <a href="/wiki/Moldova" title="Moldova">Moldova</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyzstan" title="Kyrgyzstan">Kyrgyzstan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mongolia" title="Mongolia">Mongolia</a>, Russia and <a href="/wiki/Ukraine" title="Ukraine">Ukraine</a>, and for up to 30 days to citizens of <a href="/wiki/Argentina" title="Argentina">Argentina</a>, <a href="/wiki/Brazil" title="Brazil">Brazil</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ecuador" title="Ecuador">Ecuador</a>, <a href="/wiki/Serbia" title="Serbia">Serbia</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Korea" title="South Korea">South Korea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tajikistan" title="Tajikistan">Tajikistan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Turkey" title="Turkey">Turkey</a>, <a href="/wiki/UAE" class="mw-redirect" title="UAE">UAE</a> and <a href="/wiki/Uzbekistan" title="Uzbekistan">Uzbekistan</a>. It also established a visa-free regime for citizens of 54 countries, including the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a> and <a href="/wiki/OECD#Member_countries" title="OECD">OECD member states</a>, the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">U.S.</a>, <a href="/wiki/Japan" title="Japan">Japan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a>, <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-tim_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tim-205">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-mfa.kz_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mfa.kz-206">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Green_economy">Green economy</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg/150px-Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg/225px-Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg/300px-Expo_2017_%22Nur_Alem%22_Pavilion_Glass_Pathway_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3456" data-file-height="4608" /></a><figcaption>Astana <a href="/wiki/Expo_2017" title="Expo 2017">Expo 2017</a> "Nur Alem" Pavilion</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan launched the Green Economy Plan in 2013. It committed Kazakhstan to meet 50 percent of its energy needs from alternative and renewable sources by 2050.<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> The green economy was projected to increase GDP by 3 percent and create some 500,000 jobs.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> The government set prices for energy produced from renewable sources. The price of 1 kilowatt-hour for energy produced by wind power plants was set at 22.68 tenge ($0.12), for 1 kilowatt-hour produced by small hydro-power plants 16.71 tenges ($0.09), and from biogas plants 32.23 tenges ($0.18).<sup id="cite_ref-renewableprices_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-renewableprices-209">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_direct_investment">Foreign direct investment</span></h3>
<p>Kazakhstan has attracted $330&#160;billion in foreign direct investment (FDI) from more than 120 countries since its independence.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> In 2015, the U.S. State Department said Kazakhstan was widely considered to have the best investment climate in the region.<sup id="cite_ref-2013ICS_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2013ICS-211">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> In 2014, 
President Nazarbayev signed into law tax concessions to promote foreign direct investment which included a 10-year exemption from corporation tax, an eight-year exemption from property tax, and a 10-year freeze on most other taxes.<sup id="cite_ref-taxnews_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-taxnews-212">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> Other incentives include a refund on capital investments of up to 30 percent once a production facility is in operation.<sup id="cite_ref-taxnews_212-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-taxnews-212">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2014, the <a href="/wiki/European_Bank_of_Reconstruction_and_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="European Bank of Reconstruction and Development">European Bank of Reconstruction and Development</a> (EBRD) and Kazakhstan created the partnership for Re-Energizing the Reform Process in Kazakhstan to work with international financial institutions to channel US$2.7&#160;billion provided by the Kazakh government into important sectors of Kazakhstan's economy.<sup id="cite_ref-EBRDprrp_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EBRDprrp-213">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As of May 2014, Kazakhstan had attracted $190&#160;billion in gross foreign investments since its independence in 1991 and it led the CIS countries in terms of FDI attracted per capita.<sup id="cite_ref-AstanaT_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AstanaT-214">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> One of the factors that attract foreign direct investments is country's political stability.
</p><p>The OECD 2017 Investment Policy Review noted that "great strides" had been made to open up opportunities to foreign investors and improve policy to attract FDI.<sup id="cite_ref-oecdkzpolicyreview_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oecdkzpolicyreview-215">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Banking">Banking</span></h3>
<p>The banking industry of Kazakhstan went through a boom-and-bust cycle in the early 21st century. After several years of rapid expansion in the mid-2000s, the banking industry collapsed in 2008. Several large banking groups, including <a href="/wiki/BTA_Bank" title="BTA Bank">BTA Bank</a> J.S.C. and Alliance Bank, defaulted soon thereafter. The industry shrank and was restructured, with system-wide loans dropping from 59 percent of GDP in 2007 to 39 percent in 2011. Although the Russian and Kazakhstani banking systems share some common features, there are key differences: Banks in Kazakhstan experienced a lengthy period of political stability and economic growth, which helped push Kazakhstan's banking system to a higher level of development. Banking technology and personnel qualifications were stronger in Kazakhstan. On the negative side, past stability in Kazakhstan arose from the concentration of virtually all political power in the hands of a single individual – the key factor in any assessment of system or country risk.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg/220px-TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg/330px-TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg/440px-TV-Turm_Almaty_-_3.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_competitiveness">Economic competitiveness</span></h3>
<p>In the 2020 Doing Business Report by the World Bank, Kazakhstan ranked 25th globally and as the number one best country globally for protecting minority investors’ rights.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Kazakhstan achieved its goal of entering the top 50 most competitive countries in 2013 and has maintained its position in the 2014–2015 World Economic Forum <a href="/wiki/Global_Competitiveness_Report" title="Global Competitiveness Report">Global Competitiveness Report</a> that was published at the beginning of September 2014.<sup id="cite_ref-AT1_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AT1-218">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Kazakhstan is ahead of other states in the CIS in almost all of the report's pillars of competitiveness, including institutions, infrastructure, macroeconomic environment, higher education and training, goods market efficiency, labour market development, financial market development, technological readiness, market size, business sophistication and innovation, lagging behind only in the category of health and primary education.<sup id="cite_ref-AT1_218-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AT1-218">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> The Global Competitiveness Index gives a score from 1 to 7 in each of these pillars, and Kazakhstan earned an overall score of 4.4.<sup id="cite_ref-AT1_218-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AT1-218">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Corruption">Corruption</span></h3>
<p>In 2005, the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> listed Kazakhstan as a corruption hotspot, on a par with <a href="/wiki/Angola" title="Angola">Angola</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bolivia" title="Bolivia">Bolivia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kenya" title="Kenya">Kenya</a>, <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pakistan" title="Pakistan">Pakistan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> In 2012, Kazakhstan ranked low in an index of the least corrupt countries<sup id="cite_ref-OECD1_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OECD1-220">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Forum" title="World Economic Forum">World Economic Forum</a> listed corruption as the biggest problem in doing business in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-OECD1_220-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OECD1-220">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> A 2017 OECD report on Kazakhstan indicated that Kazakhstan has reformed laws with regard to the civil service, judiciary, instruments to prevent corruption, <a href="/wiki/Access_to_information" class="mw-redirect" title="Access to information">access to information</a>, and prosecuting corruption.<sup id="cite_ref-oecdreport2017_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oecdreport2017-221">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> Kazakhstan has implemented anticorruption reforms that have been recognised by organizations like Transparency International.<sup id="cite_ref-tpi_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tpi-222">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2011 Switzerland confiscated US$48&#160;million in Kazakhstani assets from Swiss bank accounts, as a result of a bribery investigation in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-basel_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-basel-223">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> US officials believed the funds represented bribes paid by American officials to Kazakhstani officials in exchange for oil or prospecting rights in Kazakhstan. Proceedings eventually involved US$84&#160;million in the US and another US$60&#160;million in Switzerland.<sup id="cite_ref-basel_223-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-basel-223">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Investigation" title="Federal Bureau of Investigation">Federal Bureau of Investigation</a> and the Kazakh Anti-Corruption Agency signed a Mutual Legal Assistance Treaty in February 2015.<sup id="cite_ref-MLATfeb15_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MLATfeb15-224">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Transparency_International" title="Transparency International">Transparency International</a>'s 2022 <a href="/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index" title="Corruption Perceptions Index">Corruption Perceptions Index</a>, which scored 180 countries on a scale from 0 ("highly corrupt") to 100 ("very clean"), gave Kazakhstan a score of 36. When ranked by score, Kazakhstan ranked 101st among the 180 countries in the Index, where the country ranked first is perceived to have the most honest public sector.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> For comparison, the best score was 90 (ranked 1), the worst score was 12 (ranked 180), and the average score was 43.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Science_and_technology">Science and technology</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Kazakhstan" title="Science and technology in Kazakhstan">Science and technology in Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia,_2001%E2%88%922013.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia%2C_2001%E2%88%922013.svg/220px-Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia%2C_2001%E2%88%922013.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="309" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia%2C_2001%E2%88%922013.svg/330px-Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia%2C_2001%E2%88%922013.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7f/Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia%2C_2001%E2%88%922013.svg/440px-Trends_in_GERD_GDP_ratio_in_Central_Asia%2C_2001%E2%88%922013.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="316" data-file-height="444" /></a><figcaption>Trends in research expenditure in Central Asia, as a percentage of GDP, 2001–2013. Source: UNESCO Science Report: 2030 (2015), Figure 14.3.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Research remains largely concentrated in Kazakhstan's largest city and former capital, Almaty, home to 52 percent of research personnel. Public research is largely confined to institutes, with universities making only a token contribution. Research institutes receive their funding from national research councils under the umbrella of the Ministry of Education and Science. Their output, however, tends to be disconnected from market needs. In the business sector, few industrial enterprises conduct research themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-:02_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:02-227">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana,_Kazakhstan.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana%2C_Kazakhstan.JPG/150px-The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana%2C_Kazakhstan.JPG" decoding="async" width="150" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana%2C_Kazakhstan.JPG/225px-The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana%2C_Kazakhstan.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana%2C_Kazakhstan.JPG/300px-The_irradiation_experiments_at_the_ion_accelerator_DC-60._Astana%2C_Kazakhstan.JPG 2x" data-file-width="3648" data-file-height="5472" /></a><figcaption>Group of Kazakhstan physicists in collaboration with Uzbek researchers working at the ion accelerator DC-60</figcaption></figure>
<p>One of the most ambitious targets of the State Programme for Accelerated Industrial and Innovative Development adopted in 2010 is to raise the country's level of expenditure on research and development to 1 percent of GDP by 2015. By 2013, this ratio stood at 0.18 percent of GDP. It will be difficult to reach the target as long as economic growth remains strong.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The date of the event predicted near this tag has passed. (March 2021)">needs update</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Since 2005, the economy has grown faster (by 6 percent in 2013) than gross domestic expenditure on research and development, which only progressed from PPP$598&#160;million to PPP$714&#160;million between 2005 and 2013.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_228-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Innovation expenditure more than doubled in Kazakhstan between 2010 and 2011, representing KZT 235&#160;billion (<i>circa</i> US$1.6&#160;billion), or around 1.1 percent of GDP. Some 11 percent of the total was spent on research and development. This compares with about 40 to 70 percent of innovation expenditure in developed countries. This augmentation was due to a sharp rise in product design and the introduction of new services and production methods over this period, to the detriment of the acquisition of machinery and equipment, which has traditionally made up the bulk of Kazakhstan's innovation expenditure. Training costs represented just 2 percent of innovation expenditure, a much lower share than in developed countries.<sup id="cite_ref-:02_227-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:02-227">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:1_228-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Kazakhstan was ranked 79th in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-229" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-229">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 2012, President&#160;<a href="/wiki/Nursultan_Nazarbayev" title="Nursultan Nazarbayev">Nursultan Nazarbayev</a>&#160;announced the&#160;<i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Kazakhstan_2050_Strategy&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Kazakhstan 2050 Strategy (page does not exist)">Kazakhstan 2050 Strategy</a></i>&#160;with the slogan "Strong Business, Strong State." This pragmatic strategy proposes sweeping socio-economic and political reforms to hoist Kazakhstan among the top 30 economies by 2050. In this document, Kazakhstan gives itself 15 years to evolve into a knowledge economy. New sectors are to be created during each five-year plan. The first of these, covering the years 2010–2014, focused on developing industrial capacity in car manufacturing, aircraft engineering and the production of locomotives, passenger and cargo railroad cars. During the second five-year plan to 2019, the goal is to develop export markets for these products. To enable Kazakhstan to enter the world market of geological exploration, the country intends to increase the efficiency of traditional extractive sectors such as oil and gas. It also intends to develop rare earth metals, given their importance for electronics, laser technology, communication and medical equipment. The second five-year plan coincides with the development of the <i>Business 2020</i> roadmap for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), which makes provision for the allocation of grants to SMEs in the regions and for microcredit. The government and the National Chamber of Entrepreneurs also plan to develop an effective mechanism to help start-ups.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_228-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg/220px-Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg/330px-Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/59/Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg/440px-Soyuz_expedition_19_launch_pad.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1965" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Baikonur_Cosmodrome" title="Baikonur Cosmodrome">Baikonur Cosmodrome</a> is the world's oldest and largest operational <a href="/wiki/Spaceport" title="Spaceport">spaceport</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During subsequent five-year plans to 2050, new industries will be established in fields such as mobile, multi-media, nano- and space technologies, robotics, genetic engineering and alternative energy. Food processing enterprises will be developed with an eye to turning the country into a major regional exporter of beef, dairy and other agricultural products. Low-return, water-intensive crop varieties will be replaced with vegetable, oil and fodder products. As part of the shift to a "green economy" by 2030, 15% of acreage will be cultivated with water-saving technologies. Experimental agrarian and innovational clusters will be established and drought-resistant <a href="/wiki/Genetically_modified_crops" title="Genetically modified crops">genetically modified crops</a> developed.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_228-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/w/index.php?title=Kazakhstan_2050_Strategy&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Kazakhstan 2050 Strategy (page does not exist)">Kazakhstan 2050 Strategy</a> fixes a target of devoting 3 percent of GDP to research and development by 2050 to allow for the development of new high-tech sectors.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_228-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-228">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Digital Kazakhstan program was launched in 2018 to boost the country's economic growth through the implementation of digital technologies. Kazakhstan's digitization efforts generated 800 billion tenges (US$1.97 billion) in two years. The program helped create 120,000 jobs and attracted 32.8 billion tenges (US$80.7 million) of investment into the country.
</p><p>Around 82 percent of all public services became automated as part of the Digital Kazakhstan program.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Kazakhstan" title="Demographics of Kazakhstan">Demographics of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakhs</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg/220px-Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg/330px-Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg/440px-Population_pyramid_Kazakhstan_2020.jpg 2x" data-file-width="757" data-file-height="507" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Population_pyramid" title="Population pyramid">Population pyramid</a>, 2020</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg/220px-Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg/330px-Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg/440px-Central_Asia_Ethnic_en.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="2392" data-file-height="1504" /></a><figcaption>Central Asian ethnolinguistic patchwork, 1992</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/US_Census_Bureau" class="mw-redirect" title="US Census Bureau">US Census Bureau</a> International Database lists the population of Kazakhstan as 18.9&#160;million (May 2019),<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> while United Nations sources such as the 2022 revision of the World Population Prospects<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-232">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-233">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> give an estimate of 19,196,465. Official estimates put the population of Kazakhstan at 18.711&#160;million as of May 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-Негізгі_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Негізгі-234">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> In 2013, Kazakhstan's population rose to 17,280,000 with a 1.7 percent growth rate over the past year according to the Kazakhstan Statistics Agency.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 2009 population estimate is 6.8 percent higher than the population reported in the last census from January 1999. The decline in population that began after 1989 has been arrested and possibly reversed. Men and women make up 48.3 and 51.7 percent of the population, respectively.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ethnic_groups">Ethnic groups</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_demography_of_Kazakhstan" title="Ethnic demography of Kazakhstan">Ethnic demography of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<p>As of 2021, ethnic <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakhs</a> are 70.4 percent of the population and ethnic <a href="/wiki/Russians_in_Kazakhstan" title="Russians in Kazakhstan">Russians</a> are 15.5 percent.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-236">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> Other groups include <a href="/wiki/Tatars" title="Tatars">Tatars</a> (1.1 percent), <a href="/wiki/Ukrainians" title="Ukrainians">Ukrainians</a> (2.0 percent), <a href="/wiki/Uzbeks" title="Uzbeks">Uzbeks</a> (3.2 percent), <a href="/wiki/Germans" title="Germans">Germans</a> (1.2 percent), <a href="/wiki/Uyghur_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Uyghur people">Uyghurs</a> (1.5 percent), <a href="/wiki/Azerbaijanis" title="Azerbaijanis">Azerbaijanis</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dungans" class="mw-redirect" title="Dungans">Dungans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Turkic_peoples" title="Turkic peoples">Turks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Koreans" title="Koreans">Koreans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Poles_in_the_former_Soviet_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="Poles in the former Soviet Union">Poles</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lithuanians" title="Lithuanians">Lithuanians</a>. Some minorities such as <a href="/wiki/Ukrainians" title="Ukrainians">Ukrainians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Koreans" title="Koreans">Koreans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Volga_Germans" title="Volga Germans">Volga Germans</a> (0.9 percent), <a href="/wiki/Chechens" title="Chechens">Chechens</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Meskhetian_Turks" title="Meskhetian Turks">Meskhetian Turks</a>, and Russian political opponents of the regime, had been <a href="/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Population transfer in the Soviet Union">deported to Kazakhstan</a> in the 1930s and 1940s by Josef Stalin. Some of the largest Soviet <a href="/wiki/Labour_camp" class="mw-redirect" title="Labour camp">labour camps</a> (<a href="/wiki/Gulag" title="Gulag">Gulag</a>) existed in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Dzhasybay_beach.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Dzhasybay_beach.jpg/220px-Dzhasybay_beach.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Dzhasybay_beach.jpg/330px-Dzhasybay_beach.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Dzhasybay_beach.jpg/440px-Dzhasybay_beach.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2304" /></a><figcaption>Kazakhstanis on a <a href="/wiki/Lake_Jasybay" title="Lake Jasybay">Lake Jasybay</a> beach, <a href="/wiki/Pavlodar_Region" title="Pavlodar Region">Pavlodar Region</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Significant Russian immigration was also connected with the <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Lands_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Lands Campaign">Virgin Lands Campaign</a> and <a href="/wiki/Soviet_space_program" title="Soviet space program">Soviet space program</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev" title="Nikita Khrushchev">Khrushchev</a> era.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> In 1989, ethnic Russians were 37.8 percent of the population and Kazakhs held a majority in only 7 of the 20 regions of the country. Before 1991 there were about 1 million <a href="/wiki/Germans_of_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Germans of Kazakhstan">Germans in Kazakhstan</a>, mostly descendants of the <a href="/wiki/Volga_Germans" title="Volga Germans">Volga Germans</a> deported to Kazakhstan during World War II. After the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union" title="Dissolution of the Soviet Union">dissolution of the Soviet Union</a>, most of them emigrated to Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> Most members of the smaller <a href="/wiki/Pontian_Greek" class="mw-redirect" title="Pontian Greek">Pontian Greek</a> minority have emigrated to Greece. In the late 1930s thousands of <a href="/wiki/Koreans" title="Koreans">Koreans</a> in the Soviet Union were <a href="/wiki/Deportation_of_Koreans_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Deportation of Koreans in the Soviet Union">deported to Central Asia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> These people are now known as <a href="/wiki/Koryo-saram" title="Koryo-saram">Koryo-saram</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 1990s were marked by the emigration of many of the country's <a href="/wiki/Russians" title="Russians">Russians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ukrainians_in_Kazakhstan" title="Ukrainians in Kazakhstan">Ukrainians</a> and <a href="/wiki/Volga_Germans" title="Volga Germans">Volga Germans</a>, a process that began in the 1970s. This has made indigenous Kazakhs the largest ethnic group.<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup> Additional factors in the increase in the Kazakhstani population are higher birthrates and <a href="/wiki/Oralman" title="Oralman">immigration of ethnic Kazakhs</a> from China, <a href="/wiki/Mongolia" title="Mongolia">Mongolia</a>, and Russia.
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Kazakhstan" title="Languages of Kazakhstan">Languages of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<p>Kazakhstan is officially a bilingual country.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a> (part of the <a href="/wiki/Kipchak_languages" title="Kipchak languages">Kipchak sub-branch</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Turkic_languages" title="Turkic languages">Turkic languages</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> is spoken natively by 64.4 percent of the population and has the status of "state language". Russian is spoken by most Kazakhs,<sup id="cite_ref-253" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-253">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> has equal status to Kazakh as an "official language", and is used routinely in business, government, and inter-ethnic communication.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government announced in January 2015 that the <a href="/wiki/Latin_alphabet" title="Latin alphabet">Latin alphabet</a> will replace <a href="/wiki/Cyrillic" class="mw-redirect" title="Cyrillic">Cyrillic</a> as the writing system for the Kazakh language by 2025.<sup id="cite_ref-inform.kz_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inform.kz-255">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Other minority languages spoken in Kazakhstan include <a href="/wiki/Uzbek_language" title="Uzbek language">Uzbek</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ukrainian_language" title="Ukrainian language">Ukrainian</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uyghur_language" title="Uyghur language">Uyghur</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kyrgyz_language" title="Kyrgyz language">Kyrgyz</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Tatar_language" title="Tatar language">Tatar</a>. English, as well as Turkish, have gained popularity among younger people since the collapse of the Soviet Union. Education across Kazakhstan is conducted in either Kazakh, Russian, or both.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> In Nazarbayev's resignation speech of 2019, he projected that the people of Kazakhstan in the future will speak three languages (Kazakh, Russian and English).<sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Kazakhstan" title="Religion in Kazakhstan">Religion in Kazakhstan</a></div>
<div class="barbox tright" style="overflow-x: auto;">
<div style="border:1px solid silver; font-size:88%; padding:0.4em; width:auto; background: white;">
<table style="text-align:left; border-collapse:collapse; width:100%;">
<tbody><tr style="background:#ddd"><th style="text-align:center;" colspan="5">Religion in Kazakhstan, 2021<sup id="cite_ref-2021EthnicStatistics_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2021EthnicStatistics-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Viktorova_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Viktorova-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></th></tr>
