</div>
</div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg/220px-Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg/330px-Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg/440px-Zenkov_Cathedral_Winter.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1944" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ascension_Cathedral,_Almaty" title="Ascension Cathedral, Almaty">Ascension Cathedral</a> in Almaty</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg/220px-Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="124" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg/330px-Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg/440px-Khazret_Sultan_Mosque.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5504" data-file-height="3096" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Khazret_Sultan_Mosque" class="mw-redirect" title="Khazret Sultan Mosque">Khazret Sultan Mosque</a> is the biggest mosque in Kazakhstan.</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to the 2021 census, 69.3% of the population is <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Kazakhstan" title="Islam in Kazakhstan">Muslim</a>, 17.2% are <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Kazakhstan" title="Christianity in Kazakhstan">Christian</a>, 0.2% follow <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Kazakhstan" title="Religion in Kazakhstan">other religions</a> (mostly <a href="/wiki/Buddhism_in_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Buddhism in Kazakhstan">Buddhist</a> and <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Kazakhstan" title="History of the Jews in Kazakhstan">Jewish</a>), 11.01% chose not to answer, and 2.25% identify as <a href="/wiki/Atheist" class="mw-redirect" title="Atheist">atheist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-2021EthnicStatistics_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2021EthnicStatistics-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Viktorova_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Viktorova-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kazakhstan is a <a href="/wiki/Secular_state" title="Secular state">secular state</a> whose constitution guarantees religious freedoms. Article 39 of the constitution states: "Human rights and freedoms shall not be restricted in any way." Article 14 prohibits "discrimination on religious basis" and Article 19 ensures that everyone has the "right to determine and indicate or not to indicate his/her ethnic, party and religious affiliation." The Constitutional Council affirmed these rights in a 2009 declaration, which stated that a proposed law limiting the rights of certain individuals to practice their religion was declared unconstitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Kazakhstan" title="Islam in Kazakhstan">Islam</a> is the largest religion in Kazakhstan, followed by <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy_in_Kazakhstan" title="Eastern Orthodoxy in Kazakhstan">Eastern Orthodox Christianity</a>. After decades of <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_the_Soviet_Union" title="Religion in the Soviet Union">religious suppression by the Soviet Union</a>, the coming of independence witnessed a surge in the expression of ethnic identity, partly through religion. The free practice of <a href="/wiki/Religion" title="Religion">religious beliefs</a> and the establishment of full freedom of religion led to an increase of religious activity. Hundreds of mosques, churches, and other religious structures were built in the span of a few years, with the number of religious associations rising from 670 in 1990 to 4,170 today.<sup id="cite_ref-kazakhrel_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kazakhrel-259">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some figures show that <a href="/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslims" class="mw-redirect" title="Non-denominational Muslims">non-denominational Muslims</a><sup id="cite_ref-260" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-260">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup> form the majority, while others indicate that most Muslims in the country are <a href="/wiki/Sunni_Islam" title="Sunni Islam">Sunnis</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Hanafi" class="mw-redirect" title="Hanafi">Hanafi</a> school.<sup id="cite_ref-261" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-261">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> These include ethnic <a href="/wiki/Kazakhs" title="Kazakhs">Kazakhs</a>, who constitute about 70% of the population, as well as ethnic <a href="/wiki/Uzbeks" title="Uzbeks">Uzbeks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Uighurs" class="mw-redirect" title="Uighurs">Uighurs</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Tatars" title="Tatars">Tatars</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-usstate_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-usstate-262">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> Less than 1% are part of the Sunni <a href="/wiki/Shafi%60i" class="mw-redirect" title="Shafi`i">Shafi`i</a> school (primarily <a href="/wiki/Chechens" title="Chechens">Chechens</a>). There are also some <a href="/wiki/Ahmadiyya" title="Ahmadiyya">Ahmadi</a> Muslims.<sup id="cite_ref-263" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-263">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> There are a total of 2,300 mosques,<sup id="cite_ref-kazakhrel_259-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kazakhrel-259">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> all of them are affiliated with the "Spiritual Association of Muslims of Kazakhstan", headed by a supreme <a href="/wiki/Mufti" title="Mufti">mufti</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> Unaffiliated mosques are forcefully closed.<sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Eid_al-Adha" title="Eid al-Adha">Eid al-Adha</a> is recognised as a national holiday.<sup id="cite_ref-kazakhrel_259-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kazakhrel-259">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> One quarter of the population is Russian Orthodox, including ethnic Russians, Ukrainians and Belarusians.<sup id="cite_ref-USCOIRFk2009_266-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USCOIRFk2009-266">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> Other Christian groups include <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Kazakhstan" title="Catholic Church in Kazakhstan">Roman Catholics</a>, <a href="/wiki/Greek_Catholics" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Catholics">Greek Catholics</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Protestants" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestants">Protestants</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-usstate_262-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-usstate-262">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> There are a total of 258 Orthodox churches, 93 Catholic churches (9 <a href="/wiki/Greek_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Catholic">Greek Catholic</a>), and over 500 Protestant churches and prayer houses. The Russian Orthodox Christmas is recognised as a national holiday in Kazakhstan.<sup id="cite_ref-kazakhrel_259-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kazakhrel-259">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> Other religious groups include Judaism, the <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith_in_Kazakhstan" title="Baháʼí Faith in Kazakhstan">Baháʼí Faith</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hinduism_in_Kazakhstan" title="Hinduism in Kazakhstan">Hinduism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Buddhism_in_Central_Asia" title="Buddhism in Central Asia">Buddhism</a>, and <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-usstate_262-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-usstate-262">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the 2009 Census data, there are very few Christians outside the Slavic and Germanic ethnic groups.<sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Kazakhstan" title="Education in Kazakhstan">Education in Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg/220px-Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="114" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg/330px-Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg/440px-Palace_of_Arts_Shabyt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3597" data-file-height="1872" /></a><figcaption>Kazakh National University of Arts</figcaption></figure>
<p>Education is universal and mandatory through to the <a href="/wiki/Secondary_education" title="Secondary education">secondary level</a> and the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_literacy_rate" title="List of countries by literacy rate">adult literacy rate</a> is 99.5%.<sup id="cite_ref-collegeatlas_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-collegeatlas-268">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> On average, these statistics are equal to both women and men in Kazakhstan.<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Education consists of three main phases: primary education (forms 1–4), basic general education (forms 5–9) and senior level education (forms 10–11 or 12) divided into continued general education and vocational education. Vocational Education usually lasts three or four years.<sup id="cite_ref-UNEVOC_270-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNEVOC-270">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> (Primary education is preceded by one year of pre-school education.) These levels can be followed in one institution or in different ones (e.g., primary school, then secondary school). Recently, several secondary schools, specialised schools, magnet schools, <a href="/wiki/Gymnasium_(school)" title="Gymnasium (school)">gymnasiums</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lyceum#Lyceums_in_today&#39;s_education" title="Lyceum">lyceums</a> and linguistic and technical gymnasiums have been founded. Secondary professional education is offered in special professional or technical schools, lyceums or colleges and vocational schools.<sup id="cite_ref-collegeatlas_268-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-collegeatlas-268">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At present, there are <a href="/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Kazakhstan" title="List of universities in Kazakhstan">universities</a>, <a href="/wiki/Academy" title="Academy">academies</a> and institutes, <a href="/wiki/College_or_university_school_of_music" class="mw-redirect" title="College or university school of music">conservatories</a>, higher schools and higher colleges. There are three main levels: basic higher education that provides the fundamentals of the chosen field of study and leads to the award of the <a href="/wiki/Bachelor%27s_degree" title="Bachelor&#39;s degree">Bachelor's degree</a>; specialised higher education after which students are awarded the Specialist's Diploma; and scientific-pedagogical higher education which leads to the master's degree. Postgraduate education leads to the <i><a href="/wiki/Kandidat_Nauk" class="mw-redirect" title="Kandidat Nauk">Kandidat Nauk</a></i> ("Candidate of Sciences") and the Doctor of Sciences (PhD). With the adoption of the Laws on Education and on Higher Education, a private sector has been established and several private institutions have been licensed.
</p><p>Over 2,500 students in Kazakhstan have applied for student loans totalling about $9&#160;million. The largest number of student loans come from Almaty, Astana and Kyzylorda.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The training and skills development programs in Kazakhstan are also supported by international organisations. For example, on 30 March 2015, the World Banks' Group of Executive Directors approved a $100&#160;million loan for the Skills and Job project in Kazakhstan.<sup id="cite_ref-WB11_272-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WB11-272">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> The project aims to provide training to unemployed, unproductively self-employed, and employees in need of training.<sup id="cite_ref-WB11_272-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WB11-272">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Kazakhstan" title="Culture of Kazakhstan">Culture of Kazakhstan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_clothing" title="Kazakh clothing">Kazakh clothing</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_wedding_ceremony" title="Kazakh wedding ceremony">Kazakh wedding ceremony</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Equestrian_heritage,_Kazakhstan.JPEG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Equestrian_heritage%2C_Kazakhstan.JPEG/170px-Equestrian_heritage%2C_Kazakhstan.JPEG" decoding="async" width="170" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Equestrian_heritage%2C_Kazakhstan.JPEG/255px-Equestrian_heritage%2C_Kazakhstan.JPEG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Equestrian_heritage%2C_Kazakhstan.JPEG/340px-Equestrian_heritage%2C_Kazakhstan.JPEG 2x" data-file-width="1152" data-file-height="1728" /></a><figcaption>A Kazakhstani performer demonstrates the long equestrian heritage as part of the gala concert during the opening ceremonies of the Central Asian Peacekeeping Battalion.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Before the Russian colonisation, the Kazakhs had a highly developed culture based on their nomadic pastoral economy. <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> was introduced into the region with the arrival of the <a href="/wiki/Arabs" title="Arabs">Arabs</a> in the 8th century. It initially took hold in the southern parts of <a href="/wiki/Turkestan" title="Turkestan">Turkestan</a> and spread northward.<sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Samanids" class="mw-redirect" title="Samanids">Samanids</a> helped the religion take root through zealous missionary work. The <a href="/wiki/Golden_Horde" title="Golden Horde">Golden Horde</a> further propagated Islam amongst the tribes in the region during the 14th century.<sup id="cite_ref-274" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-274">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GUWSatpayev.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/GUWSatpayev.jpg/170px-GUWSatpayev.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/GUWSatpayev.jpg/255px-GUWSatpayev.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/49/GUWSatpayev.jpg/340px-GUWSatpayev.jpg 2x" data-file-width="734" data-file-height="1113" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Kanysh_Satpayev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kanysh Satpayev">Kanysh Satpayev</a>, one of the founders of Soviet-era <a href="/wiki/Metallogeny" title="Metallogeny">metallogeny</a>, principal advocate and the first president of <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_Academy_of_Sciences" title="Kazakhstan Academy of Sciences">Kazakhstan Academy of Sciences</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan is home to a large number of prominent contributors to literature, science and philosophy: <a href="/wiki/Abay_Qunanbayuli" class="mw-redirect" title="Abay Qunanbayuli">Abay Qunanbayuli</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mukhtar_Auezov" title="Mukhtar Auezov">Mukhtar Auezov</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gabit_Musirepov" title="Gabit Musirepov">Gabit Musirepov</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kanysh_Satpayev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kanysh Satpayev">Kanysh Satpayev</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mukhtar_Shakhanov" title="Mukhtar Shakhanov">Mukhtar Shakhanov</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saken_Seyfullin" class="mw-redirect" title="Saken Seyfullin">Saken Seyfullin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jambyl_Jabayev" class="mw-redirect" title="Jambyl Jabayev">Jambyl Jabayev</a>, among many others.
</p><p>Tourism is a rapidly growing industry in Kazakhstan and it is joining the international tourism networking. In 2010, Kazakhstan joined The Region Initiative (TRI) which is a Tri-regional Umbrella of Tourism-related organisations. TRI is functioning as a link between three regions: South Asia, Central Asia, and Eastern Europe. Armenia, Bangladesh, Georgia, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, India, Nepal, Pakistan, Russia, Sri Lanka, Tajikistan, Turkey, and Ukraine are now partners, and Kazakhstan is linked with other South Asian, Eastern European, and Central Asian countries in the tourism market.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_literature" title="Kazakh literature">Kazakh literature</a></div>
<p>Kazakh literature is defined as "the body of literature, both <a href="/wiki/Oral_literature" title="Oral literature">oral</a> and written, produced in the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh language</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Kazakh people">Kazakh people</a> of Central Asia".<sup id="cite_ref-Feldman_275-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldman-275">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> Kazakh literature expands from the current territory of Kazakhstan, also including the era of <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic" title="Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic">Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic</a>, Kazakh recognised territory under the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_Khanate" title="Kazakh Khanate">Kazakh Khanate</a>. There is some overlap with several complementary themes, including the literature of Turkic tribes that inhabited Kazakhstan over the course of its history and literature written by ethnic Kazakhs.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg/220px-USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg/330px-USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg/440px-USSR_stamp_A.Qunanbayuli_1965_4k.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1756" data-file-height="1272" /></a><figcaption>1965 <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet</a> stamp honouring Kazakh essayist and poet <a href="/wiki/Abai_Qunanbaiuly" title="Abai Qunanbaiuly">Abai Qunanbaiuly</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>According to Chinese written sources from the 6th–8th centuries CE, the Turkic tribes of Kazakhstan had an oral poetry tradition. These came from earlier periods and were primarily transmitted by <a href="/wiki/Bard" title="Bard">bards</a>: professional storytellers and musical performers.<sup id="cite_ref-Nurmanova_276-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nurmanova-276">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> Traces of this tradition are shown on <a href="/wiki/Old_Turkic_alphabet" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Turkic alphabet">Orkhon script</a> stone carvings dated 5th–7th centuries CE that describe rule of Kultegin and Bilge, two early Turkic rulers ("kagans").<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="We need a source to reference this information. (October 2017)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Amongst the Kazakhs, the bard was a primarily, though not exclusively, male profession. Since at least the 17th century, Kazakh bards could be divided into two main categories: the zhıraws (zhiraus, žyraus), who passed on the works of others, usually not creating and adding their own original work; and the <a href="/wiki/Aqyn" title="Aqyn">aqyns</a> (akyns), who improvised or created their own poems, stories or songs.<sup id="cite_ref-Feldman_275-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldman-275">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> There were several types of works, such as <a href="/wiki/Didactic" class="mw-redirect" title="Didactic">didactic</a> <i>termes</i>, <a href="/wiki/Elegiac" title="Elegiac">elegiac</a> <i>tolgaws</i>, and <a href="/wiki/Epic_(genre)" title="Epic (genre)">epic</a> <i>zhırs</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Feldman_275-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldman-275">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> Although the origins of such tales are often unknown, most of them were associated with bards of the recent or more distant past, who supposedly created them or passed them on, by the time most Kazakh poetry and prose was first written down in the second half of the 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-Feldman_275-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldman-275">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> There are clear stylistic differences between works first created in the 19th century, and works dating from earlier periods but not documented before the 19th century, such as those attributed to such 16th- and 17th-century bards as Er Shoban and Dosmombet Zhıraw (also known as Dospambet Žyrau; he appeared to have been literate, and reportedly visited <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>), and even to such 15th-century bards as Shalkiz and Asan Qayghı.<sup id="cite_ref-Feldman_275-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldman-275">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other notable bards include Kaztugan Žyrau, Žiembet Žyrau, Axtamberdy Žyrau, and Buxar Žyrau Kalkamanuly, who was an advisor to <a href="/wiki/Ablai_Khan" title="Ablai Khan">Ablai Khan</a>, and whose works have been preserved by Mäšhür Žüsip Köpeev.<sup id="cite_ref-Nurmanova_276-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nurmanova-276">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> <i>Er Targhın</i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Alpamysh" title="Alpamysh">Alpamıs</a></i> are two of the most famous examples of Kazakh literature to be recorded in the 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-Feldman_275-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Feldman-275">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Book_of_Dede_Korkut" title="Book of Dede Korkut">Book of Dede Korkut</a></i> and Oguz Name (a story of an ancient Turkic king <a href="/wiki/Oghuz_Khan" class="mw-redirect" title="Oghuz Khan">Oghuz Khan</a>) are the most well-known Turkic heroic legends. Initially created around the 9th century CE, they were passed on through generations in oral form. The legendary tales were recorded by Turkish authors in 14–16th centuries C.E.<sup id="cite_ref-277" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-277">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-278" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-278">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The preeminent role in the development of modern literary Kazakh belongs to <a href="/wiki/Abai_Qunanbaiuly" title="Abai Qunanbaiuly">Abai Qunanbaiuly</a> (<a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a>: <span lang="kk">Абай Құнанбайұлы</span>, sometimes Russified to Abay Kunanbayev, Абай Кунанбаев) (1845–1904), whose writings did much to preserve Kazakh folk culture. Abai's major work is <i>The Book of Words</i> (<a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a>: <span lang="kk">қара сөздері, Qara sözderi</span>), a philosophical treatise and collection of poems where he criticises Russian colonial policies and encourages other Kazakhs to embrace education and literacy. The literary magazines <i><a href="/wiki/Ay_Qap" title="Ay Qap">Ay Qap</a></i> (published between 1911 and 1915 in Arabic script) and <i>Qazaq</i> (published between 1913 and 1918) played an important role in the development of the intellectual and political life among early 20th-century Kazakhs.<sup id="cite_ref-279" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-279">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Kazakhstan" title="Music of Kazakhstan">Music of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg/220px-Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg/330px-Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg/440px-Stamp_of_Kazakhstan_659.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1188" data-file-height="903" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Nowruz" title="Nowruz">Nowruz</a> on stamp of Kazakhstan</figcaption></figure>
<p>The modern state of Kazakhstan is home to the Kazakh State Kurmangazy Orchestra of Folk Instruments, the Kazakh State Philharmonic Orchestra, the Kazakh National Opera and the Kazakh State Chamber Orchestra. The folk instrument orchestra was named after <a href="/wiki/Kurmangazy_Sagyrbayuly" class="mw-redirect" title="Kurmangazy Sagyrbayuly">Kurmangazy Sagyrbayuly</a>, a famous composer and dombra player from the 19th century. The Musical-Dramatic Training College, founded in 1931, was the first institute of higher education for music. Two years later, the Orchestra of Kazakh Folk Musical Instruments was formed.<sup id="cite_ref-280" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-280">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup>
The Foundation Asyl Mura is archiving and publishing historical recordings of great samples of Kazakh music both traditional and classical. The leading conservatoire is in Almaty, the Qurmanghazy Conservatoire. It competes with the national conservatoire in Astana, Kazakhstan's capital.
</p><p>When referring to traditional Kazakh music, authentic folklore must be separated from "folklorism". The latter denotes music executed by academically trained performers who aim at preserving the traditional music for coming generations. As far as can be reconstructed, the music of Kazakhstan from the period before a strong Russian influence consists of instrumental music and vocal music. Instrumental music, with the pieces ("Küy") being performed by soloists. Text is often seen in the background (or "program") for the music, as a lot of Küy titles refer to stories. Vocal music, either as part of a ceremony such as a wedding (mainly performed by women), or as part of a feast. Here we might divide into subgenres: epic singing, containing not only historical facts, but as well the tribe's genealogy, love songs, and didactic verses; and as a special form the composition of two or more singers in public (Aitys), of dialogue character and usually unexpectedly frankly in content.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Astudio.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Astudio.jpg/220px-Astudio.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Astudio.jpg/330px-Astudio.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Astudio.jpg/440px-Astudio.jpg 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/A-Studio" title="A-Studio">A-Studio</a> was created in 1982 in <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a>, then called Alma-Ata, hence called "Alma-Ata Studio".</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Russian influence on the music life in Kazakhstan can be seen in two spheres: first, the introduction of musical academic institutions such as concert houses with opera stages, and conservatories, where European music was performed and taught, and second, by trying to incorporate Kazakh traditional music into these academic structures. Controlled first by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a> and then the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>, Kazakhstan's folk and classical traditions became connected with <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_Russian_music" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethnic Russian music">ethnic Russian music</a> and Western European music. Prior to the 20th century, Kazakh folk music was collected and studied by <a href="/wiki/Ethnography" title="Ethnography">ethnographic</a> research teams including composers, music critics and <a href="/wiki/Musicology" title="Musicology">musicologists</a>. In the first part of the 19th century, Kazakh music was transcribed in linear <a href="/wiki/Musical_notation" title="Musical notation">notation</a>. Some composers of this era set Kazakh folk songs to Russian-style <a href="/wiki/European_classical_music" class="mw-redirect" title="European classical music">European classical music</a>.
</p><p>The Kazakhs themselves, however, did not write their own music in notation until 1931. Later, as part of the Soviet Union, Kazakh folk culture was encouraged in a sanitised manner designed to avoid political and social unrest. The result was a bland derivative of real Kazakh folk music. In 1920, <a href="/wiki/Aleksandr_Zatayevich" title="Aleksandr Zatayevich">Aleksandr Zatayevich</a>, a Russian official, created major works of art music with melodies and other elements of Kazakh folk music. Beginning in 1928 and accelerating in the 1930s, he also adapted traditional Kazakh instruments for use in Russian-style ensembles, such as by increasing the number of <a href="/wiki/Fret" title="Fret">frets</a> and <a href="/wiki/Strings_(music)" class="mw-redirect" title="Strings (music)">strings</a>. Soon, these styles of modern orchestral playing became the only way for musicians to officially play; Kazakh folk was turned into patriotic, professional and socialist endeavours.<sup id="cite_ref-281" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-281">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fine_arts">Fine arts</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_art" title="Kazakh art">Kazakh art</a></div>
<p>In Kazakhstan, the fine arts, in the classical sense, have their origins in the second half of the 19th century and the beginning of the 20th century. It was largely influenced by Russian artists, such as Vasily Vereshchagin and Nikolai Khludov, who intensively travelled in Central Asia. Khludov had a particular influence on the development of the local school of painting, becoming the teacher of many local artists. The most famous of these is Abilkhan Kasteyev, after whom the State Museum of Art of Kazakhstan was renamed in 1984.<sup id="cite_ref-282" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-282">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Kazakh school of fine arts was fully formed by the 1940s and flourished in the 1950s. Local painters, graphic artists and sculptors, trained under the unified Soviet system of artist education, began active work, often using national motifs in their art. The painters O. Tansykbaev, J. Shardenov, K. Telzhanov, and S. Aitbaev, graphic artists E. Sidorkina and A. Duzelkhanov, and sculptors H. Nauryzbaeva and E. Sergebaeva are today counted among the key figures of Kazakhstani art.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kazakh_cuisine" title="Kazakh cuisine">Kazakh cuisine</a></div>
<p>In the national cuisine, livestock meat, like <a href="/wiki/Horse_meat" title="Horse meat">horse meat</a><sup id="cite_ref-283" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-283">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Beef" title="Beef">beef</a> can be cooked in a variety of ways and is usually served with a wide assortment of traditional bread products. Refreshments include black tea, often served with milk and dried fruits (such as dried apricots) and nuts. In southern provinces, people often prefer <a href="/wiki/Green_tea" title="Green tea">green tea</a>. Traditional milk-derived drinks such as <a href="/wiki/Ayran" title="Ayran">ayran</a>, <a href="/wiki/Shubat" class="mw-redirect" title="Shubat">shubat</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kymyz" class="mw-redirect" title="Kymyz">kymyz</a>. A traditional Kazakh dinner involves a multitude of appetisers on the table, followed by a soup and one or two main courses such as <a href="/wiki/Pilaf" title="Pilaf">pilaf</a> and <a href="/wiki/Beshbarmak" title="Beshbarmak">beshbarmak</a>. They also drink their national beverage, <a href="/wiki/Kumis" title="Kumis">kumys</a>, which consists of fermented mare's milk.<sup id="cite_ref-284" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-284">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sport">Sport</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sport_in_Kazakhstan" title="Sport in Kazakhstan">Sport in Kazakhstan</a></div>
<p>Kazakhstan consistently performs in Olympic competitions. It is especially successful in <a href="/wiki/Boxing" title="Boxing">boxing</a>. This has brought some attention to the Central Asian nation and increased world awareness of its athletes. <a href="/wiki/Dmitry_Karpov" class="mw-redirect" title="Dmitry Karpov">Dmitry Karpov</a> and <a href="/wiki/Olga_Rypakova" title="Olga Rypakova">Olga Rypakova</a> are among the most notable Kazakhstani athletes. <a href="/wiki/Dmitry_Karpov" class="mw-redirect" title="Dmitry Karpov">Dmitry Karpov</a> is a distinguished decathlete, taking bronze in both the <a href="/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics" title="2004 Summer Olympics">2004 Summer Olympics</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/2003_World_Championships_in_Athletics" title="2003 World Championships in Athletics">2003</a> and <a href="/wiki/2007_World_Athletics_Championships" class="mw-redirect" title="2007 World Athletics Championships">2007 World Athletics Championships</a>. <a href="/wiki/Olga_Rypakova" title="Olga Rypakova">Olga Rypakova</a> is an athlete, specialising in <a href="/wiki/Triple_jump" title="Triple jump">triple jump</a> (women's), taking silver in the <a href="/wiki/2011_World_Championships_in_Athletics" title="2011 World Championships in Athletics">2011 World Championships in Athletics</a> and Gold in the <a href="/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics" title="2012 Summer Olympics">2012 Summer Olympics</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg/220px-FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="134" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg/330px-FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg/440px-FC_Astana_vs._Celtic_FC_UCL_2017.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3024" data-file-height="1840" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Astana_Arena" title="Astana Arena">Astana Arena</a> opened in 2009.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan's city of <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a> submitted bids twice for the <a href="/wiki/Winter_Olympics" class="mw-redirect" title="Winter Olympics">Winter Olympics</a>: in <a href="/wiki/Almaty_bid_for_the_2014_Winter_Olympics" title="Almaty bid for the 2014 Winter Olympics">2014</a> and again for the <a href="/wiki/2022_Winter_Olympics" title="2022 Winter Olympics">2022 Winter Olympics</a>. <a href="/wiki/Astana" title="Astana">Astana</a> and <a href="/wiki/Almaty" title="Almaty">Almaty</a> hosted the <a href="/wiki/2011_Asian_Winter_Games" title="2011 Asian Winter Games">2011 Asian Winter Games</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-285" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-285">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Popular sports in Kazakhstan include football, basketball, ice hockey, bandy, and boxing.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">Football</a> is the most popular sport in Kazakhstan. The <a href="/wiki/Football_Federation_of_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Football Federation of Kazakhstan">Football Federation of Kazakhstan</a> is the sport's national governing body. The FFK organises the <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_national_football_team" title="Kazakhstan national football team">men's</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_women%27s_national_football_team" title="Kazakhstan women&#39;s national football team">women's</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Futsal" title="Futsal">futsal</a> national teams.
</p><p>Kazakhstan's most famous basketball player was <a href="/wiki/Alzhan_Zharmukhamedov" title="Alzhan Zharmukhamedov">Alzhan Zharmukhamedov</a>, who played for <a href="/wiki/PBC_CSKA_Moscow" title="PBC CSKA Moscow">CSKA Moscow</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union%27s_national_basketball_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Soviet Union&#39;s national basketball team">Soviet Union's national basketball team</a> in the 1960s and 1970s. Throughout his career, he won multiple titles and medals at some of the world's most prestigious basketball competitions, including the <a href="/wiki/Basketball_at_the_Summer_Olympics" title="Basketball at the Summer Olympics">Summer Olympics</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Basketball_World_Cup" class="mw-redirect" title="Basketball World Cup">Basketball World Cup</a>, the <a href="/wiki/EuroBasket" title="EuroBasket">EuroBasket</a> (the European Basketball Championship), and the <a href="/wiki/EuroLeague" title="EuroLeague">EuroLeague</a>. In 1971 he earned the title <a href="/wiki/Unified_Sports_Classification_System_of_the_USSR" class="mw-redirect" title="Unified Sports Classification System of the USSR">Master of Sports of the USSR, International Class</a> and a year later he was awarded the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Badge_of_Honor" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of the Badge of Honor">Order of the Badge of Honor</a>. <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan%27s_national_basketball_team" class="mw-redirect" title="Kazakhstan&#39;s national basketball team">Kazakhstan's national basketball team</a> was established in 1992, after the dissolution of the Soviet Union. Since its foundation, it has been competitive at the continental level. Its greatest accomplishment was at the <a href="/wiki/Basketball_at_the_2002_Asian_Games_%E2%80%93_Men" title="Basketball at the 2002 Asian Games – Men">2002 Asian Games</a>, where it defeated the <a href="/wiki/Philippines_men%27s_national_basketball_team" title="Philippines men&#39;s national basketball team">Philippines</a> in its last game to win the bronze medal. At the official <a href="/wiki/Asian_Basketball_Championship" class="mw-redirect" title="Asian Basketball Championship">Asian Basketball Championship</a>, now called FIBA Asia Cup, the Kazakhs' best finish was 4th place in 2007.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_national_bandy_team" title="Kazakhstan national bandy team">Kazakhstan national bandy team</a> is among the best in the world, and has many times won the bronze medal at the <a href="/wiki/Bandy_World_Championship" title="Bandy World Championship">Bandy World Championship</a>, including the <a href="/wiki/2012_Bandy_World_Championship" title="2012 Bandy World Championship">2012 edition</a> when Kazakhstan hosted the tournament on home ice.<sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-287" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-287">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/2011_Bandy_World_Championship" title="2011 Bandy World Championship">2011 tournament</a>, they were an extra-time in the semi-final from reaching the final for the first time. In 2012, they were even closer when they took it to a penalty shootout. The team won the first <a href="/wiki/Bandy_at_the_2011_Asian_Winter_Games" title="Bandy at the 2011 Asian Winter Games">bandy tournament at the Asian Winter Games</a>. During the Soviet time, Dynamo Alma-Ata won the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Russian_bandy_champions" title="List of Russian bandy champions">Soviet Union national championships</a> in 1977 and 1990 and the <a href="/wiki/European_Cup_(bandy)" title="European Cup (bandy)">European Cup</a> in 1978. Bandy is developed in ten of the country's seventeen administrative divisions (eight of the fourteen regions and two of the three cities which are situated inside of but are not part of regions).<sup id="cite_ref-288" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-288">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Akzhaiyk" class="mw-redirect" title="Akzhaiyk">Akzhaiyk</a> from <a href="/wiki/Oral,_Kazakhstan" title="Oral, Kazakhstan">Oral</a>, however, is the only professional club.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG/150px-Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG" decoding="async" width="150" height="187" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG/225px-Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG/300px-Nik_Antropov_Jets_2012-02-11.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1539" data-file-height="1922" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Nikolai_Antropov" class="mw-redirect" title="Nikolai Antropov">Nikolai Antropov</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_men%27s_national_ice_hockey_team" title="Kazakhstan men&#39;s national ice hockey team">Kazakh national ice hockey team</a> have competed in <a href="/wiki/Ice_hockey" title="Ice hockey">ice hockey</a> in the 1998 and <a href="/wiki/2006_Winter_Olympics" title="2006 Winter Olympics">2006 Winter Olympics</a>, as well as in the <a href="/wiki/2006_Men%27s_World_Ice_Hockey_Championships" class="mw-redirect" title="2006 Men&#39;s World Ice Hockey Championships">2006 Men's World Ice Hockey Championships</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_Hockey_Championship" title="Kazakhstan Hockey Championship">Kazakhstan Hockey Championship</a> is held since 1992. <a href="/wiki/Barys_Astana" title="Barys Astana">Barys Astana</a> is the main domestic Kazakhstani ice hockey professional team, and having played in the Kazakhstani national league until the 2008–09 season, when they were transferred to play in the <a href="/wiki/Kontinental_Hockey_League" title="Kontinental Hockey League">Kontinental Hockey League</a>. Meanwhile, the <a href="/wiki/Kazzinc-Torpedo" title="Kazzinc-Torpedo">Kazzinc-Torpedo</a> and play in the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Hockey_League" title="Supreme Hockey League">Supreme Hockey League</a> since 1996 and the <a href="/wiki/Saryarka_Karagandy" title="Saryarka Karagandy">Saryarka Karagandy</a> since 2012. Top Kazakhstani ice hockey players include <a href="/wiki/Nik_Antropov" title="Nik Antropov">Nik Antropov</a>, Ivan Kulshov and <a href="/wiki/Evgeni_Nabokov" title="Evgeni Nabokov">Evgeni Nabokov</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Kazakhstan_national_amateur_boxing_athletes" title="Kazakhstan national amateur boxing athletes">Kazakh boxers</a> are generally well known in the world. In the last three Olympic Games, their performance was assessed as one of the best and they had more medals than any country in the world, except <a href="/wiki/Cuba" title="Cuba">Cuba</a> and Russia (in all three games). In 1996 and 2004, three Kazakhstani boxers (<a href="/wiki/Vassiliy_Jirov" title="Vassiliy Jirov">Vassiliy Jirov</a> in 1996, <a href="/wiki/Bakhtiyar_Artayev" title="Bakhtiyar Artayev">Bakhtiyar Artayev</a> in 2004 and <a href="/wiki/Serik_Sapiyev" title="Serik Sapiyev">Serik Sapiyev</a> in 2012) were recognised as the best boxers for their techniques with the <a href="/wiki/Val_Barker_Trophy" class="mw-redirect" title="Val Barker Trophy">Val Barker Trophy</a>, awarded to the best boxer of the tournament. In <a href="/wiki/Boxing" title="Boxing">boxing</a>, Kazakhstan performed well in the <a href="/wiki/2000_Summer_Olympics" title="2000 Summer Olympics">2000 Summer Olympics</a> in Sydney. Two boxers, <a href="/wiki/Bekzat_Sattarkhanov" title="Bekzat Sattarkhanov">Bekzat Sattarkhanov</a> and <a href="/wiki/Yermakhan_Ibraimov" title="Yermakhan Ibraimov">Yermakhan Ibraimov</a>, earned <a href="/wiki/Gold_medal" title="Gold medal">gold medals</a>. Another two boxers, <a href="/wiki/Bulat_Zhumadilov" title="Bulat Zhumadilov">Bulat Zhumadilov</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mukhtarkhan_Dildabekov" title="Mukhtarkhan Dildabekov">Mukhtarkhan Dildabekov</a>, earned <a href="/wiki/Silver_medal" title="Silver medal">silver medals</a>. <a href="/wiki/Oleg_Maskaev" title="Oleg Maskaev">Oleg Maskaev</a>, born in <a href="/wiki/Taraz" title="Taraz">Zhambyl</a>, representing Russia, was the <a href="/wiki/World_Boxing_Council" title="World Boxing Council">WBC</a> Heavyweight Champion after knocking out <a href="/wiki/Hasim_Rahman" title="Hasim Rahman">Hasim Rahman</a> on 12 August 2006. The reigning <a href="/wiki/World_Boxing_Association" title="World Boxing Association">WBA</a>, WBC, IBF and <a href="/wiki/International_Boxing_Organization" title="International Boxing Organization">IBO</a> <a href="/wiki/List_of_middleweight_boxing_champions" class="mw-redirect" title="List of middleweight boxing champions">middleweight champion</a> is Kazakh boxer <a href="/wiki/Gennady_Golovkin" title="Gennady Golovkin">Gennady Golovkin</a>. <a href="/wiki/Natascha_Ragosina" title="Natascha Ragosina">Natascha Ragosina</a>, representing Russia, but from <a href="/wiki/Karaganda" title="Karaganda">Karaganda</a> held seven versions of the women's super middleweight title, and two heavyweight titles during her boxing career. She holds the record as the longest-reigning WBA female super middleweight champion, and the longest-reigning WBC female super middleweight champion.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Film">Film</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cinema_of_Kazakhstan" title="Cinema of Kazakhstan">Cinema of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg/220px-Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg/330px-Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b1/Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg/440px-Red_Carpet_at_AIAFF.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4288" data-file-height="2848" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/International_Astana_Action_Film_Festival" title="International Astana Action Film Festival">International Astana Action Film Festival</a>, 2010</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan's film industry is run through the state-owned <a href="/wiki/Kazakhfilm" title="Kazakhfilm">Kazakhfilm</a> studios based in Almaty. The studio has produced award-winning movies such as <i><a href="/wiki/Myn_Bala" title="Myn Bala">Myn Bala</a></i>, <i><a href="/wiki/Harmony_Lessons" title="Harmony Lessons">Harmony Lessons</a></i>, and <i>Shal</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-289" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-289">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> Kazakhstan is the host of the <a href="/wiki/International_Astana_Action_Film_Festival" title="International Astana Action Film Festival">International Astana Action Film Festival</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Eurasia_International_Film_Festival" title="Eurasia International Film Festival">Eurasia International Film Festival</a> held annually. Hollywood director <a href="/wiki/Timur_Bekmambetov" title="Timur Bekmambetov">Timur Bekmambetov</a> is from Kazakhstan and has become active in bridging Hollywood to the Kazakhstan film industry.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (February 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Kazakhstan journalist Artur Platonov won Best Script for his documentary "Sold Souls" about Kazakhstan's contribution to the struggle against terrorism at the 2013 Cannes Corporate Media and TV Awards.<sup id="cite_ref-290" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-290">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Serik Aprymov's <i>Little Brother</i> (<i>Bauyr</i>) won at the Central and Eastern Europe Film Festival <a href="/wiki/GoEast" title="GoEast">goEast</a> from the German Federal Foreign Office.<sup id="cite_ref-EEC_292-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EEC-292">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Media">Media</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Media_of_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Media of Kazakhstan">Media of Kazakhstan</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg/150px-Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg/225px-Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg/300px-Timur_Bekmambetov_by_Gage_Skidmore.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2646" data-file-height="3168" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Timur_Bekmambetov" title="Timur Bekmambetov">Timur Bekmambetov</a>, a notable Kazakh director</figcaption></figure>
<p>Kazakhstan is ranked 161 out of 180 countries on the <a href="/wiki/World_Press_Freedom_Index" title="World Press Freedom Index">World Press Freedom Index</a>, compiled by <a href="/wiki/Reporters_Without_Borders" title="Reporters Without Borders">Reporters Without Borders</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-293" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-293">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> A mid-March 2002 <a href="/wiki/Court_order" title="Court order">court order</a>, with the government as a <a href="/wiki/Plaintiff" title="Plaintiff">plaintiff</a>, stated that <i><a href="/wiki/Respublika_(Kazakh_newspaper)" title="Respublika (Kazakh newspaper)">Respublika</a></i> were to stop printing for three months.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_294-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-294">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> The order was evaded by printing under other titles, such as <i>Not That Respublika</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_294-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-294">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> In early 2014, a court also issued a cease publication order to the small-circulation Assandi-Times newspaper, saying it was a part of the Respublika group. Human Rights Watch said: "this absurd case displays the lengths to which Kazakh authorities are willing to go to bully critical media into silence."<sup id="cite_ref-295" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-295">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With support from the US Department of State's Bureau for Democracy, Human Rights and Labor (DRL), the American Bar Association Rule of Law Initiative opened a media support centre in Almaty to bolster free expression and journalistic rights in Kazakhstan.<sup id="cite_ref-aba_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aba-296">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="UNESCO_World_Heritage_sites">UNESCO World Heritage sites</span></h3>
<p>Kazakhstan has three cultural and two natural sites on the <a href="/wiki/UNESCO" title="UNESCO">UNESCO</a> World Heritage list. The cultural sites are:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Mausoleum_of_Khoja_Ahmed_Yasawi" title="Mausoleum of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi">Mausoleum of Khoja Ahmed Yassaui</a>, added in 2003</li>
<li>Petroglyphs within the Archaeological Landscape of <a href="/wiki/Tamgaly" class="mw-redirect" title="Tamgaly">Tamgaly</a>, added in 2004</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Silk_Roads:_the_Routes_Network_of_Chang%27an-Tianshan_Corridor" title="Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang&#39;an-Tianshan Corridor">Silk Roads: the Routes Network of Chang'an-Tianshan Corridor</a>, added in 2014</li></ul>
<p>The natural sites are:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Saryarka_-_Steppe_and_Lakes_of_Northern_Kazakhstan" class="mw-redirect" title="Saryarka - Steppe and Lakes of Northern Kazakhstan">Saryarka - Steppe and Lakes of Northern Kazakhstan</a>, added in 2008</li>
<li>Western <a href="/wiki/Tien_Shan" class="mw-redirect" title="Tien Shan">Tien Shan</a>, added in 2016<sup id="cite_ref-UNESCOkz_297-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNESCOkz-297">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Public_holidays">Public holidays</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Kazakhstan" title="Public holidays in Kazakhstan">Public holidays in Kazakhstan</a></div>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Explanatory_notes">Explanatory notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-6"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-6">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"Kazakhstani" refers to all citizens of Kazakhstan, regardless of ethnicity.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-11"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-11">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">dd.mm.yyyy in Russian.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-12"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-12">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><div><ul><li><a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a>: <span lang="kk">Қазақстан</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Kazakh-language romanization"><i lang="kk-Latn">Qazaqstan</i></span>, <small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="kk-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Kazakh" title="Help:IPA/Kazakh">[qɑzɑqsˈtɑn]</a></span></li><li><a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a>: <span lang="ru">Казахстан</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Russian" title="Romanization of Russian">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Russian-language romanization"><i lang="ru-Latn">Kazakhstan</i></span>, <small>pronounced&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ru-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Russian" title="Help:IPA/Russian">[kəzɐxˈstan]</a></span></li></ul></div></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-13"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-13">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><div><ul><li><a href="/wiki/Kazakh_language" title="Kazakh language">Kazakh</a>: <span lang="kk">Қазақстан Республикасы</span>, <small>romanized:&#160;</small><span title="Kazakh-language romanization"><i lang="kk-Latn">Qazaqstan Respublikasy</i></span></li><li><a href="/wiki/Russian_language" title="Russian language">Russian</a>: <span lang="ru">Республика Казахстан</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Russian" title="Romanization of Russian">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Russian-language romanization"><i lang="ru-Latn">Respublika Kazakhstan</i></span></li></ul></div></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">About 4% of Kazakhstan's territory, west of the <a href="/wiki/Ural_(river)" title="Ural (river)">Ural River</a>, lies in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Europe" title="Eastern Europe">Eastern Europe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Factbook-Ural_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Factbook-Ural-15">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<li id="cite_note-32"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-32">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to Allentoft et al. (2015) and Haak et al. (2015),</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Beckwith49-33"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Beckwith49_33-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFBeckwith2009">Beckwith 2009</a>, p.&#160;49<span class="error harv-error" style="display: none; font-size:100%"> harvnb error: no target: CITEREFBeckwith2009 (<a href="/wiki/Category:Harv_and_Sfn_template_errors" title="Category:Harv and Sfn template errors">help</a>)</span>: "Archaeologists are now generally agreed that the Andronovo culture of the Central Steppe region in the second millennium BC is to be equated with the Indo-Iranians."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-B_68-34"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-B_68_34-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFBeckwith2009">Beckwith 2009</a>, p.&#160;68<span class="error harv-error" style="display: none; font-size:100%"> harvnb error: no target: CITEREFBeckwith2009 (<a href="/wiki/Category:Harv_and_Sfn_template_errors" title="Category:Harv and Sfn template errors">help</a>)</span> "Modern scholars have mostly used the name Saka to refer to Iranians of the Eastern Steppe and Tarim Basin"</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-D_37-35"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-D_37_35-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFDandamayev1994">Dandamayev 1994</a>, p.&#160;37<span class="error harv-error" style="display: none; font-size:100%"> harvnb error: no target: CITEREFDandamayev1994 (<a href="/wiki/Category:Harv_and_Sfn_template_errors" title="Category:Harv and Sfn template errors">help</a>)</span> "In modern scholarship the name 'Sakas' is reserved for the ancient tribes of northern and eastern Central Asia and Eastern Turkestan to distinguish them from the related Massagetae of the Aral region and the Scythians of the Pontic steppes. These tribes spoke Iranian languages, and their chief occupation was nomadic pastoralism."</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-106"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-106">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFIwiński2006" class="citation web cs1">Iwiński, Tadeusz (7 July 2006). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://assembly.coe.int/nw/xml/XRef/X2H-Xref-ViewHTML.asp?FileID=9998">"Situation in Kazakhstan and its relations with the Council of Europe"</a>. <i>Council of Europe – Parliamentary Assembly</i>. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://webcache.googleusercontent.com/search?q=cache:qT4wuwEEvisJ:assembly.coe.int/nw/xml/XRef/X2H-Xref-ViewHTML.asp%3FFileID%3D9998+&amp;cd=1&amp;hl=en&amp;ct=clnk&amp;gl=us&amp;client=firefox-b-1-d">Archived</a> from the original on 15 January 2020<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">14 February</span> 2020</span>. <q>For its part, the Assembly might decide, already at this stage, to interpret Rule 59 of its Rules of procedure, concerning special guests, in such a way as to include the Eurasian States. Two Council of Europe member States, Turkey and Russia, belong geographically to both Europe and Asia and are therefore Eurasian. Strictly speaking, the three South Caucasus States, Armenia, Azerbaijan and Georgia are located in Asia, yet their membership of political Europe is no longer in doubt. [...] This being the case, Kazakhstan, as a Eurasian State participating in the OSCE and a signatory to the International covenant on civil and political rights and the International covenant on economic, social and cultural rights, would meet the criteria laid down in Rule 59, making it eligible to apply for special guest status with the Assembly.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Council+of+Europe+%E2%80%93+Parliamentary+Assembly&amp;rft.atitle=Situation+in+Kazakhstan+and+its+relations+with+the+Council+of+Europe&amp;rft.date=2006-07-07&amp;rft.aulast=Iwi%C5%84ski&amp;rft.aufirst=Tadeusz&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fassembly.coe.int%2Fnw%2Fxml%2FXRef%2FX2H-Xref-ViewHTML.asp%3FFileID%3D9998&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AKazakhstan" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-107"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-107">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFCornellEngvall2017" class="citation web cs1">Cornell, Svante; Engvall, Johan (2017). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://isdp.eu/content/uploads/2017/10/2017-cornell-engvall-kazakhstan-in-europe-why-not.pdf">"Kazakhstan in Europe: Why Not?"</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. <i>Institute for Security &amp; Development Policy</i>. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://web.archive.org/web/20201024154030/https://isdp.eu/content/uploads/2017/10/2017-cornell-engvall-kazakhstan-in-europe-why-not.pdf">Archived</a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span> from the original on 24 October 2020<span class="reference-accessdate">.
